**Istanbul**[^cite_note-naming-10] is the [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_cities_and_towns_in_Turkey) in [Turkey](11125639.md), a [megacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megacity), constituting the country's economic, cultural, and historical center. With [a population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Istanbul) of over 15 million, it is home to 18% of the [population of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Turkey).[^cite_note-Population_of_Turkey-4] Istanbul is among the [largest cities in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_cities_by_population_within_city_limits)[^cite_note-11] and [in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_proper_by_population) by population. It is a city on two continents; about two-thirds of its population live in [Europe](9239.md) and the rest in [Asia](689.md).[^cite_note-12] Istanbul straddles the [Bosphorus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosphorus) – one of the world's busiest waterways – in northwestern Turkey, between the [Sea of Marmara](28863.md) and the [Black Sea](3386.md). Its area of 5,461 square kilometers (2,109 mi<sup>2</sup>) is coterminous with Istanbul Province.[^cite_note-13]

The city developed to become one of the most significant cities in history. [Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantium) was founded on the (present-day) [Sarayburnu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarayburnu) promontory by [Greek colonists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_colonisation) in the seventh century BC.[^cite_note-Herrin2009-14] Over nearly 16 centuries following its reestablishment as [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) in 330 AD, it served as the capital of four empires: the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) (330–395), the [Eastern Roman (Byzantine) Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) (395–1204 and 1261–1453), the [Latin Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Empire) (1204–1261), and the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) (1453–1922).[^cite_note-15] It was instrumental in the advancement of Christianity during Roman and Byzantine times, before the Ottomans [conquered the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople) in 1453 and transformed it into an Islamic stronghold and the seat of its [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Caliphate).[^cite_note-maag1145-16] Although the Republic of Turkey established its capital in [Ankara](802.md), palaces and imperial mosques still line Istanbul's hills as visible reminders of the city's previous central role. [The historic centre of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_Areas_of_Istanbul) is a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md).[^cite_note-17]

Istanbul's strategic position along the historic [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road),[^cite_note-18] rail networks to Europe and [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia), and the only sea route between the Black Sea and the [Mediterranean](19006.md) have helped foster an eclectic populace, although less so since the establishment of the Republic in 1923. Overlooked for the new capital during the interwar period, the city has since regained much of its prominence. The population of the city has increased tenfold since the 1950s, as migrants from across [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) have flocked to the metropolis and city limits have expanded to accommodate them.[^cite_note-tu224-19][^cite_note-ecoc-popdem-20] The majority of Istanbul’s Turkish citizens are of ethnic Turkish origin, whereas ethnic [Kurds](17068.md) represent the largest ethnic minority in the city.

Considered an alpha [global city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_city),[^cite_note-21] Istanbul accounts for about thirty percent of [Turkey's economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Turkey).[^cite_note-TUIK_GDP-5] The Istanbul-[İzmit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmit) area is one of the main industrial regions in Turkey.[^cite_note-22] In 2025, [Euromonitor International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromonitor_International) ranked Istanbul as [the fifth most visited city in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_by_international_visitors).[^cite_note-23] Istanbul is home to two international airports, multiple ports, and [numerous universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Istanbul).[^cite_note-24] It is among the top 100 science and technology clusters in the world.[^cite_note-25] The city hosts a large part of [Turkish football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Turkey) and sports in general, with clubs such as [Galatasaray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galatasaray_S.K.), [Fenerbahçe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fenerbah%C3%A7e_S.K.) and [Beşiktaş](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Be%C5%9Fikta%C5%9F_J.K.). Istanbul is vulnerable to earthquakes as it is in close proximity to the [North Anatolian Fault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Anatolian_Fault).[^cite_note-26]

## Names

Main article: [Names of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_Istanbul)

The first known name of the city is *[Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantium)* ([Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language): Βυζάντιον, *Byzántion*), the name given to it at its foundation by [Megarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megarian) colonists around 657 BC.[^cite_note-Britannica-Istanbul-27][^cite_note-roo177-28] Megarian colonists claimed a direct line back to the founders of the city, Byzas, the son of the god Poseidon and the nymph Ceroëssa.[^cite_note-roo177-28] Modern excavations have raised the possibility that the name Byzantium might reflect the sites of native [Thracian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thracian) settlements that preceded the fully-fledged town.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgacas1947352ff-29] [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): Κωνσταντινούπολις, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek): *Kōnstantinoupolis*; [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language): *Constantinopolis*) comes from the Latin name Constantinus, after [Constantine the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great), the Roman emperor who refounded the city in 324 AD.[^cite_note-roo177-28] Constantine had initially called the city [New Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Rome) ([Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language): Νέα Ῥώμη; *Nea Rhomē*; [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language): *Nova Roma*).[^cite_note-Britannica-Istanbul-27] Constantinople remained the most common name for the city in the West until the 1930s, when Turkish authorities began to press for the use of *Istanbul* in foreign languages. *Ḳosṭanṭīnīye* ([Ottoman Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turkish_language): قسطنطينيه) and *İstanbul* were the names used alternatively by the Ottomans during their rule.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENecipoğlu2010262-30]

The name *İstanbul* ([Ottoman Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turkish_language): استانبول; [\[isˈtanbuɫ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Turkish) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/f/fa/Istanbul_pronunciation.ogg/Istanbul_pronunciation.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/f/fa/Istanbul_pronunciation.ogg/Istanbul_pronunciation.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup> or colloquially pronounced [\[ɯsˈtambuɫ\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Turkish)) is commonly held to derive from the [Medieval Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Greek) phrase *eis tḕn [Pólin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polis)* (εἰς τὴν Πόλιν, pronounced [\[is tim ˈbolin\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Greek)), literally "to the city",[^cite_note-31] and is how Constantinople was referred to by the local Greeks. This reflected its status as the only major city in the vicinity. The importance of Constantinople in the Ottoman world was also reflected by its nickname *Dersaadet* ([Ottoman Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turkish_language): درساعدت) meaning the 'Gate to Prosperity' in Ottoman Turkish.[^cite_note-32] An alternative view is that the name evolved directly from "Constantinople", with the first and third syllables dropped.[^cite_note-roo177-28] Some Ottoman sources of the 17th century, such as [Evliya Çelebi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evliya_%C3%87elebi), describe it as the common Turkish name of the time; between the late 17th and late 18th centuries, it was also in official use. The first use of the word *Islambol* ([Ottoman Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turkish_language): اسلامبول) on coinage was in 1730 during the reign of Sultan [Mahmud I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_I).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkel200557,_383-33] In modern [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language), the name is written as *İstanbul*, with a dotted İ, as the [Turkish alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_alphabet) distinguishes between a [dotted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0) and [dotless I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dotless_I). In English, the stress is on the first or last syllable, but in Turkish it is on the second syllable.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGökselKerslake200527-34] A person from the city is an *İstanbullu* (plural *İstanbullular*); *Istanbulite* is used in English.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyder199995-35]

## History

Main article: [History of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Istanbul)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Istanbul history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Istanbul_history).

[Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) artifacts, uncovered by archeologists at the beginning of the 21st century, indicate that Istanbul's historic peninsula was settled as far back as the 6th millennium BC.[^cite_note-BBC-Rainsford-2009-36] That early settlement, important in the spread of the [Neolithic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic_Revolution) from the Near East to Europe, lasted for almost a millennium before being inundated by rising water levels.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-BBC-Rainsford-2009-36][^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] The first human settlement on the Asian side, the Fikirtepe mound, is from the [Copper Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper_Age) period, with artifacts dating from 5500 to 3500 BC,[^cite_note-40] On the European side, near the point of the peninsula ([Sarayburnu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarayburnu)), there was a Thracian settlement during the early 1st millennium BC. Modern authors have linked it to the Thracian toponym *Lygos*,[^cite_note-janin-41] mentioned by [Pliny the Elder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pliny_the_Elder) as an earlier name for the site of Byzantium.[^cite_note-42]

The history of the city proper begins around 660 BC,[^cite_note-Britannica-Istanbul-27][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBloomBlair20091-43][^cite_note-byz-date-49] when Greek settlers from Megara established Byzantium on the European side of the Bosporus. The settlers built an [acropolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acropolis) adjacent to the [Golden Horn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horn) on the site of the early Thracian settlements, fueling the nascent city's economy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEÇelik199311-50] The city experienced a brief period of [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire) rule at the turn of the 5th century BC, but the Greeks recaptured it during the [Greco-Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Persian_Wars).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDe_Souza200388-51] Byzantium then continued as part of the [Athenian League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delian_League) and its successor, the [Second Athenian League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Athenian_League), before gaining independence in 355 BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreely199620-52] Long allied with the Romans, Byzantium officially became a part of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) in 73 AD.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreely199622-53] Byzantium's decision to side with the [Roman usurper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_usurper) [Pescennius Niger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pescennius_Niger) against Emperor [Septimius Severus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septimius_Severus) cost it dearly; by the time it surrendered at the end of 195 AD, two years of siege had left the city devastated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrant19968–10-54] Five years later, Severus began to rebuild Byzantium, and the city regained – and, by some accounts, surpassed – its previous prosperity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELimberis199411–12-55]

### Byzantine era



Originally built by Constantine the Great in the 4th century and later rebuilt by [Justinian I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justinian_I) after the [Nika riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nika_riots) in 532, the [Hagia Irene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Irene) is an [Eastern Orthodox Church](10186.md) located in the outer courtyard of [Topkapı Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topkap%C4%B1_Palace) in Istanbul. It is one of the few Byzantine era churches that were never converted into mosques; during the Ottoman period it served as Topkapı's principal armoury

Constantine the Great effectively became the emperor of the whole of the Roman Empire in September 324.[^cite_note-56] Two months later, he laid out the plans for a new, Christian city to replace Byzantium. As the eastern capital of the empire, the city was named *[Nova Roma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Rome)*; most called it Constantinople, a name that persisted into the 20th century.[^cite_note-57] On 11 May 330, Constantinople was proclaimed the capital of the Roman Empire, which was later permanently divided between the two sons of [Theodosius I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodosius_I) upon his death on 17 January 395, when the city became the capital of the eastern empire. During the following millennium of Roman history the state was commonly referred to as the "[Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire)".[^cite_note-ba222-58]

The establishment of Constantinople was one of Constantine's most lasting accomplishments, shifting Roman power eastward as the city became a center of Greek culture and Christianity.[^cite_note-ba222-58][^cite_note-gr63-59] Numerous churches were built across the city, including [Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia) which was built during the reign of [Justinian I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justinian_I) and remained the world's largest cathedral for a thousand years.[^cite_note-klwa171-60] Constantine also undertook a major renovation and expansion of the [Hippodrome of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippodrome_of_Constantinople); accommodating tens of thousands of spectators, the hippodrome became central to civic life and, in the 5th and 6th centuries, the center of episodes of unrest, including the [Nika riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nika_riots).[^cite_note-61][^cite_note-62] Constantinople's location also ensured its existence would stand the test of time; for many centuries, its walls and seafront protected Europe against invaders from the east and the advance of Islam.[^cite_note-gr63-59] During most of the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), the latter part of the Byzantine era, Constantinople was the largest and wealthiest city on the European continent and at times the largest in the world.[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64] Constantinople is generally considered to be the center and the "cradle of Orthodox [Christian civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_civilization)".[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66]



The 6th century [Basilica Cistern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_Cistern) was built by [Justinian the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justinian_I)

Constantinople began to decline continuously after the end of the reign of [Basil II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basil_II) in 1025. The [Fourth Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Crusade) was diverted from its purpose in 1204, and the city was sacked and pillaged by the crusaders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGregory2010324–29-67] They established the [Latin Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Empire) in place of the Orthodox Byzantine Empire.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGregory2010330–333-68] Hagia Sophia was converted to a Catholic church in 1204. The Byzantine Empire was restored, albeit weakened, in 1261.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGregory2010340-69] Constantinople's churches, defenses, and basic services were in disrepair,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGregory2010341–342-70] and its population had dwindled to a hundred thousand from half a million during the 8th century.[^cite_note-pop-figures-71] After the reconquest of 1261, however, some of the city's monuments were restored, and some, like the two [Deesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deesis) mosaics in Hagia Sophia and Kariye, were created.[^cite_note-72]

Various economic and military policies instituted by [Andronikos II Palaiologos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andronikos_II_Palaiologos), such as the reduction of military forces, weakened the empire and left it vulnerable to attack.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReinert2002258–260-73] In the mid-14th-century, the [Ottoman Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turks) began a strategy of gradually taking smaller towns and cities, cutting off Constantinople's supply routes and strangling it slowly.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaynes194947-74] On 29 May 1453, after a 55-day siege during which the last Roman emperor, [Constantine XI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_XI_Palaiologos), was killed, Sultan [Mehmed II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_II) "the Conqueror" [captured Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople).[^cite_note-75]

### Ottoman Empire

Main article: [Istanbul during the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_during_the_Ottoman_Empire)



[Topkapı Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topkap%C4%B1_Palace) was built by Sultan [Mehmed II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_II) between 1459 and 1465

Sultan Mehmed declared Constantinople the new capital of the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md). Hours after the fall of the city, the sultan rode to the Hagia Sophia and summoned an imam to proclaim the *[shahada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahada)*, converting the grand cathedral into an imperial mosque due to the city's refusal to surrender peacefully.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGregory2010394–399-76] Mehmed declared himself as the new *[Kayser-i Rûm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kayser-i_R%C3%BBm)*, the Ottoman Turkish equivalent of the [Caesar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesar_\(title\)) of Rome, and the Ottoman state was reorganized into an empire.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBéhar199938-77][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBideleuxJeffries199871-78]



16th century map of Istanbul by the Ottoman polymath [Matrakçı Nasuh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matrak%C3%A7%C4%B1_Nasuh)

Following the capture of Constantinople, [Mehmed II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_II) immediately set out to revitalize the city. Cognizant that revitalization would fail without the repopulation of the city, Mehmed II welcomed everyone–foreigners, criminals, and runaways– showing extraordinary openness and willingness to incorporate outsiders that came to define Ottoman political culture.[^cite_note-79] He also invited people from all over Europe to his capital, creating a cosmopolitan society that persisted through much of the Ottoman period.[^cite_note-80] Revitalizing Istanbul also required a massive program of restorations, of everything from roads to [aqueducts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valens_Aqueduct#Ottoman_period).[^cite_note-hughes2020-81] Like many monarchs before and since, Mehmed II transformed Istanbul's urban landscape with the wholesale redevelopment of the city center.[^cite_note-madden2020-82] There was a [huge new palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topkap%C4%B1_Palace) to rival, if not overshadow, [the old one](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Palace_of_Constantinople), a new covered market (still standing as the [Grand Bazaar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Bazaar,_Istanbul)), porticoes, pavilions, walkways, as well as more than a dozen new mosques.[^cite_note-hughes2020-81] Mehmed II turned the ramshackle old town into something that looked like an imperial capital.[^cite_note-madden2020-82]

[Social hierarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_stratification) was ignored by the rampant plague, which killed the rich and the poor alike in the 16th century.[^cite_note-blackd-83] Money could not protect the rich from all the discomforts and harsher sides of Istanbul.[^cite_note-blackd-83] Although the Sultan lived at a safe remove from the masses, and the wealthy and poor tended to live side by side, for the most part Istanbul was not zoned as modern cities are.[^cite_note-blackd-83] Opulent houses shared the same streets and districts with tiny hovels.[^cite_note-blackd-83] Those rich enough to have secluded country properties had a chance of escaping the periodic epidemics of sickness that blighted Istanbul.[^cite_note-blackd-83]



View of the [Golden Horn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horn) and the [Seraglio Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seraglio_Point) from [Galata Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galata_Tower)

The [Ottoman dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_dynasty) claimed the status of [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate) in 1517, with Constantinople remaining the capital of this [last caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Caliphate) for four centuries.[^cite_note-maag1145-16] [Suleiman the Magnificent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suleiman_the_Magnificent)'s reign from 1520 to 1566 was a period of especially great artistic and architectural achievement; chief architect [Mimar Sinan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimar_Sinan) designed several iconic buildings in the city, while Ottoman arts of [ceramics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0znik_pottery), [stained glass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stained_glass), [calligraphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_calligraphy), and [miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_miniature) flourished.[^cite_note-84] The population of Constantinople was 570,000 by the end of the 18th century.[^cite_note-chfo-85]

A period of rebellion at the start of the 19th century led to the rise of the progressive Sultan [Mahmud II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud_II) and eventually to the *[Tanzimat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanzimat)* period, which produced political reforms and allowed new technology to be introduced to the city.[^cite_note-86] Bridges across the Golden Horn were constructed during this period,[^cite_note-87] and Constantinople was connected to the rest of the European railway network in the 1880s.[^cite_note-ha251-88] Modern facilities, such as a water supply network, electricity, telephones, and trams, were gradually introduced to Constantinople over the following decades, although later than to other European cities.[^cite_note-89] The modernization efforts were not enough to forestall the [decline of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_of_the_Ottoman_Empire).[^cite_note-90]



[Cadde-i Kebir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stiklal_Avenue) in 1912 (present-day [İstiklal Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stiklal_Avenue) in [Beyoğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyo%C4%9Flu)). The entrance of the [Church of St. Anthony of Padua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_St._Anthony_of_Padua,_Istanbul) is seen at left. A [Nestlé](160227.md) advertisement is visible on a building in the background.

With the [Young Turk Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Turk_Revolution) in 1908, the [Ottoman Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Assembly_of_the_Ottoman_Empire), closed since 14 February 1878, was reopened 30 years later on 23 July 1908, which marked the beginning of the [Second Constitutional Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Constitutional_Era).[^cite_note-tarihiolaylar-91] The civil strife and political uncertainties in the Ottoman Empire during the months after the revolution encouraged [Austria-Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austria-Hungary) to [annex Bosnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_Crisis) and [Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Bulgaria) to [declare its independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgarian_Declaration_of_Independence) in a jointly coordinated move on 5 October 1908. Sultan [Abdul Hamid II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Hamid_II) was deposed in 1909, following the counter-revolution attempt known as the [31 March incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/31_March_incident). A series of wars in the early 20th century, such as the [Italo-Turkish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italo-Turkish_War) (1911–1912) and the [Balkan Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Wars) (1912–1913), plagued the ailing empire's capital and resulted in the [1913 Ottoman coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1913_Ottoman_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), which brought the regime of the [Three Pashas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Pashas).[^cite_note-92]

The Ottoman Empire joined [World War I](4764461.md) (1914–1918) on the side of the [Central Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Powers) and was ultimately defeated. The [deportation of Armenian intellectuals on 24 April 1915](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deportation_of_Armenian_intellectuals_on_24_April_1915) was among the major events which marked the start of the [Armenian genocide](140376.md) during WWI.[^cite_note-93] Due to Ottoman and Turkish policies of [Turkification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkification) and [ethnic cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing), the city's [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_Ottoman_Empire) population declined from 450,000 to 240,000 between 1914 and 1927.[^cite_note-94] During World War I, between 1916 and 1918, a number of [aerial bombing raids by British aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombing_of_Constantinople_in_World_War_I) took place in or near Istanbul.[^cite_note-95][^cite_note-96] The [Armistice of Mudros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armistice_of_Mudros) was signed on 30 October 1918.[^cite_note-97] Less than a month later, on 12 November 1918, a ship carrying a French brigade entered the city, beginning the [occupation of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Constantinople). It was followed by a fleet consisting of British, Italian, Greek, and French ships deploying soldiers on the ground the next day. The subsequent [occupation of Smyrna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Smyrna) ([İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir)), which was back then the second-largest Ottoman city, on 15 May 1919 also marked the beginning of the [Turkish War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_War_of_Independence) led by [Mustafa Kemal Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Kemal_Atat%C3%BCrk) and the [Turkish National Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_National_Movement), who convened in [Erzurum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erzurum_Congress) and [Sivas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sivas_Congress) for consolidating the resistance groups in [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia). The [Ottoman Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Parliament) was dissolved by the [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) on 11 April 1920, upon which many of its former members joined the Turkish National Movement headquartered in [Ankara](802.md), where the [Grand National Assembly of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_National_Assembly_of_Turkey) was established on 23 April 1920. Less than four months later, the representatives of the Istanbul-based Ottoman government led by [Damat Ferid Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damat_Ferid_Pasha) signed the [Treaty of Sèvres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_S%C3%A8vres) on 10 August 1920, which was rejected by the GNAT in Ankara.[^cite_note-98]

Following the [Turkish War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_War_of_Independence) (1919–1922), the [Grand National Assembly of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_National_Assembly_of_Turkey) in Ankara abolished the Sultanate on 1 November 1922, and the last Ottoman Sultan, [Mehmed VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehmed_VI), was declared *[persona non grata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/150_personae_non_gratae_of_Turkey)*. Leaving aboard the British warship [HMS *Malaya*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HMS_Malaya) on 17 November 1922, he went into exile and died in [Sanremo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanremo), Italy, on 16 May 1926.



A view of [Bankalar Caddesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bankalar_Caddesi) (*Banks Street*) in the late 1920s. Completed in 1892, the [Ottoman Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Bank) headquarters is seen at left. In 1995 the [Istanbul Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borsa_Istanbul) moved to [İstinye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stinye), while numerous Turkish banks have moved to [Levent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levent) and [Maslak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maslak)

[^cite_note-99]

The [Treaty of Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lausanne) was signed on 24 July 1923, and the occupation of Constantinople ended with the departure of the last forces of the Allies from the city on 4 October 1923.[^cite_note-liberation-of-istanbul-100] Turkish forces of the Ankara government, commanded by [Şükrü Naili Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9E%C3%BCkr%C3%BC_Naili_G%C3%B6kberk) (3rd Corps), entered the city with a ceremony on 6 October 1923, which has been marked as the "Liberation Day of Istanbul" (*İstanbul'un Kurtuluşu*), and has been commemorated annually since.[^cite_note-liberation-of-istanbul-100]

### Turkish Republic



[Karaköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karak%C3%B6y) [ferry pier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferries_in_Istanbul) in the 1930s

On 29 October 1923 the [Grand National Assembly of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_National_Assembly_of_Turkey) declared the establishment of the Turkish Republic, with [Ankara](802.md) as its capital. [Mustafa Kemal Atatürk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustafa_Kemal_Atat%C3%BCrk) became the Republic's first [President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Turkey).[^cite_note-101][^cite_note-102] The name *[Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople)*, which remained in use for 1600 years, was officially changed as *İstanbul* on 28 March 1930; a decision which inspired the lyrics of the 1953 song *[Istanbul (Not Constantinople)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_\(Not_Constantinople\))*, written on the 500th anniversary of the [conquest of the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople) by the [Ottoman Turks](22278.md) in 1453. Other city names that were changed with the Postal Services Law ([Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language): *Posta Hizmetleri Kanunu*) of 28 March 1930 include *[Angora](802.md#Etymology)* which was renamed as *[Ankara](802.md)*, and *[Smyrna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir#Names_and_etymology)* which was renamed as *[İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir)*, among numerous other examples of internationally used city names that were replaced by officially standardized Turkish names.



[Taksim Square](631961.md), c. 1936

From the 1930s onward, with large-scale urban planning efforts initially led by [Henri Prost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Prost) between 1936 and 1950 and continued by his colleagues such as [Luigi Piccinato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luigi_Piccinato) in later years, Istanbul underwent great structural change, as new public squares, boulevards, avenues and parks were constructed throughout the city, often at the expense of historical buildings; such as [Taksim Square](631961.md), [Taksim Gezi Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taksim_Gezi_Park), Vatan Caddesi, Atatürk Bulvarı, Kemeraltı Caddesi, Meclis-i Mebusan Caddesi, [Barbaros Bulvarı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbaros_Boulevard), Cumhuriyet Caddesi and Tarlabaşı Bulvarı, among others.[^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104]

The [1942 wealth tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varl%C4%B1k_Vergisi) during [World War II](32927.md), which was levied with much higher rates on non-Muslims, led to the transfer or liquidation of many businesses owned by religious minorities.[^cite_note-105] The [1955 Istanbul pogrom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_pogrom), during which numerous Greeks (with various estimates) were killed, wounded or sexually assaulted, led to the emigration of most of the remaining Greeks in Istanbul.[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-zayas-107] Further [mass expulsions of Istanbulite Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_Greeks_from_Istanbul) took place in 1964–1965, when those who were [officially citizens of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_Greeks_from_Istanbul#Population_affected) but were allowed to stay in Turkey according to the [Treaty of Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lausanne) and were exempted from the [population exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_exchange_between_Greece_and_Turkey) were ordered to promptly leave the country, including those with a spouse who held Turkish citizenship.[^cite_note-o112-108] The decision was made after Turkey and Greece came to the brink of war in 1964 over [violent clashes in Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cypriot_intercommunal_violence#Intercommunal_violence), which was averted through the intervention by U.S. president [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson), who wanted to prevent a conflict between two [NATO](21133.md) members at the height of the [Cold War](325329.md). As a result of these policies, the Greek population of Istanbul decreased from 110,000 in 1919 to 2,500 by the end of the 20th century.[^cite_note-m310-109]



A view of the [fourth Galata Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galata_Bridge#The_fourth_bridge) (1912–1992) in 1966, with the restoration works (1965–1967)

[^cite_note-Galata_Tower_1-110]

&#x20;of the [conical roof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conical_roof) of [Galata Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galata_Tower) (the previous one was removed in the 19th century)

[^cite_note-Galata_Tower_1-110]

[^cite_note-Galata_Tower_2-111]

[^cite_note-Galata_Tower_3-112]

[^cite_note-116]

&#x20;visible at left.

Turkey suffered from political turmoil and economic crises between [1960](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_Turkish_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) and [1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Turkish_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), and the emigration of Istanbul's ethnic and religious minorities, mainly to [Europe](9239.md) and [North America](21139.md), continued in this period; while new laws with the aim of [Turkification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkification) were introduced, such as the nationalization or closure of private higher education institutions in [1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Turkish_military_memorandum).[^cite_note-Grigoriadis-Canpolat-117][^cite_note-Yellice-118] As a result, the [college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College) section of [Robert College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_College) (RC) in Istanbul, which offered 4-year [bachelor's degree](188874.md) programs (e.g. the programs of the School of Engineering) and [master's degree](170346.md) programs (e.g. in Industrial Engineering), was nationalized and reorganized as [Boğaziçi University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bo%C4%9Fazi%C3%A7i_University) in 1971; while its [high school](554992.md) section, formerly named Robert Academy (RA), continues to operate as an American high school, with the Turkish name *Robert Kolej* referring mainly to the still active high school section since 1971.[^cite_note-Grigoriadis-Canpolat-117][^cite_note-Yellice-118] Applying the same law of 1971, the [Turkish Ministry of National Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_National_Education_\(Turkey\)) proposed to attach the [Theological School of Halki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halki_seminary) ([Heybeliada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heybeliada)) to the Faculty of Theology (*İlahiyat Fakültesi*) at [Istanbul University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_University), but the [Greek Orthodox Patriarchate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarchate_of_Constantinople), citing the [Treaty of Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lausanne), refused to accept this change and decided to keep the school closed until the restoration of its previous legal status, which was annulled on 9 July 1971.[^cite_note-Yellice-118] The autonomy of Turkish universities, granted with Article 120 of the [Turkish Constitution of 1961](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Constitution_of_1961),[^cite_note-119] was suspended in [1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Turkish_military_memorandum) and annulled in 1981.[^cite_note-Resmi_Gazete_06.11.1981-120] The [Council of Higher Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Higher_Education_\(Turkey\)), established on 6 November 1981[^cite_note-Resmi_Gazete_06.11.1981-120] when Turkey was administered by the [National Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Council_\(Turkey,_1980\)) chaired by General [Kenan Evren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenan_Evren) between [1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Turkish_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) and [1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Turkish_general_election), is responsible for supervising the [universities in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Turkey), in a capacity defined by Article 130 of the [Turkish Constitution of 1982](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Turkey).[^cite_note-121] Civilian rule was restored with the [1983 general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Turkish_general_election).



The [Bosphorus Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosphorus_Bridge) in 1973

The overall population of Istanbul began to rapidly increase in the 1970s, as people from [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia) migrated to the city to find employment in the many new factories that were built on the outskirts of the sprawling metropolis. This sudden, sharp rise in the city's population caused a large demand for housing, and many previously outlying villages and forests became engulfed into the [metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_area) of Istanbul as a result of [urban sprawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_sprawl).[^cite_note-122] The [Bosphorus Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosphorus_Bridge), which connects the city's European and Asian parts, was opened in 1973 and significantly contributed to the increase in migration from Anatolia. It was followed by other bridges and tunnels which span the [Bosphorus](3705.md) [strait](58646.md), namely the [Fatih Sultan Mehmet Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatih_Sultan_Mehmet_Bridge) (1988), [Marmaray Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marmaray_Tunnel) (2013), [Yavuz Sultan Selim Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yavuz_Sultan_Selim_Bridge) (2016), and [Eurasia Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia_Tunnel) (2016).



[Bağdat Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%C4%9Fdat_Avenue) is the main shopping street in the Asian side

Built in the latter half of the 1980s, the *Sahilyolu* (Coastal Road), a scenic road along the [Marmara](28863.md) coast of the Anatolian side of the city, parallel to [Bağdat Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%C4%9Fdat_Avenue) and facing the [Princes' Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princes'_Islands) (with the part between [Caddebostan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caddebostan) and [Bostancı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bostanc%C4%B1) named as the Çetin Emeç Bulvarı, and the part between Bostancı and [Pendik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pendik) named as the Turgut Özal Bulvarı), had a major impact on the growth and development of the districts in the city's Asian side. [Istanbul Sea Buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0DO), operating between the city's European and Asian sides, were also introduced in this period, with the first generation of 10 fast catamaran ferries built by Kvaerner Fjellstrand in [Norway](21241.md) entering service in 1987.&#x20;

In 1990 the pedestrianization of [İstiklal Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stiklal_Avenue) in [Beyoğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyo%C4%9Flu) was completed and the [nostalgic trams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_nostalgic_tramways) were reintroduced. Tarlabaşı Bulvarı in Beyoğlu was built in the same period as a new route for diverting the motor vehicle traffic which previously flowed along İstiklal Avenue before its pedestrianization.



[Istanbul Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Metro)'s [M8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M8_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) line is among the new lines with fully automated [driverless trains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automatic_train_operation) and [platform screen doors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platform_screen_doors) for increasing passenger safety

The [M1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M1_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) line of the [Istanbul Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Metro) was opened in 1989. It was followed by the [M2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M2_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) (2000), [M4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M4_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) (2012), [M3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M3_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) (2013), [M6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M6_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) (2015), [M5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M5_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) (2017), [M7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M7_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) (2020), [M9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M9_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) (2021), [M8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M8_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) (2023) and [M11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M11_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) (2023) [lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Metro#Lines). The [M10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M10_\(Istanbul_Metro\)), [M12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M12_\(Istanbul_Metro\)), [M13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M13_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) and [M14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M14_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) lines are currently under construction. The lines on the European and Asian sides of the city are connected by the [Marmaray Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marmaray_Tunnel) underneath the [Bosphorus](3705.md) [strait](58646.md).

[Atatürk Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atat%C3%BCrk_Airport), originally named [Yeşilköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ye%C5%9Filk%C3%B6y) Airport, was built in 1912 as an airfield for the [Ottoman Aviation Squadrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Aviation_Squadrons). In 1933 it became a civilian airport and the hub of [Turkish Airlines](167925.md) for domestic flights. The first international flight, between Istanbul and [Athens](1216.md), took place in 1947. In 1953 the airport terminal building was opened; it was the location of a few scenes in the 1963 [*James Bond*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_James_Bond_films) film *[From Russia with Love](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/From_Russia_with_Love_\(film\))*. A new international terminal building was constructed by [TAV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TAV_Airports_Holding) between 1998 and 2004, and the previous building became the domestic flights terminal. Atatürk Airport remained as Istanbul's main international airport until the end of passenger flights and the closure of the passenger terminals in 2019 (cargo flights ended in 2022). Since 2019, [Istanbul Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Airport), the current hub of [Turkish Airlines](167925.md) and one of the largest airports in the world, is the main international airport in the city's European side. [Sabiha Gökçen International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabiha_G%C3%B6k%C3%A7en_International_Airport), the main airport in the city's Asian side, was opened in 2001.

In the late 20th and early 21st centuries, Istanbul became the new home of immigrants, residents and refugees from various countries, such as [Bulgaria](3415.md), [Bosnia and Herzegovina](3463.md), [Serbia](29265.md), [Kosovo](17391.md), [North Macedonia](23564616.md), the [Turkic states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_Turkic_States) of the [Caspian](19653787.md) basin, [Russia](25391.md), [Ukraine](31750.md), [Moldova](19260.md), the [Arab states of the Persian Gulf](3088555.md), [Syria](7515849.md), [Iran](14653.md), [Afghanistan](737.md), [Pakistan](23235.md), [Libya](17633.md), [Sudan](27421.md), [Eritrea](17238590.md), [Somalia](27358.md), [Ethiopia](187749.md), [Kenya](188171.md), [Senegal](27278.md), [Ghana](12067.md), [Nigeria](21383.md) and numerous other countries mainly in [Europe](9239.md), [Asia](689.md) and [Africa](5334607.md).

## Geography and environment

Further information: [Geography of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Turkey) and [Geology of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_Turkey)

Satellite image showing a thin piece of land, densely populated on the south, bisected by a waterway

Satellite view of Istanbul and the Bosporus

Istanbul is in north-western Turkey and straddles the [Bosporus Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosporus_Strait), which provides the only passage from the [Black Sea](3386.md) to the [Mediterranean](19006.md) via the [Sea of Marmara](28863.md).[^cite_note-hisdic-123] Historically, the city has been ideally situated for trade and defense: The confluence of the Sea of Marmara, the Bosporus, and the Golden Horn provide both ideal defense against enemy attack and a natural toll-gate.[^cite_note-hisdic-123] Several picturesque islands – [Büyükada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%BCy%C3%BCkada), [Heybeliada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heybeliada), [Burgazada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burgazada), [Kınalıada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%B1nal%C4%B1ada), and five smaller islands – are part of the city.[^cite_note-hisdic-123] Istanbul's shoreline has grown beyond its natural limits. Large sections of [Caddebostan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caddebostan) sit on areas of landfill, increasing the total area of the city to 5,343 square kilometers (2,063 sq mi).[^cite_note-hisdic-123]

Despite the myth that [seven hills make up the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_hills_of_Istanbul), there are, in fact, more than 50 hills within the city limits. Istanbul's tallest hill, Aydos, is 537 meters (1,762 ft) high.[^cite_note-hisdic-123]

### Earthquakes

See also: [Architecture of Turkey § Earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Turkey#Earthquakes)

The [North Anatolian Fault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Anatolian_Fault), under the [Sea of Marmara](28863.md), is [locked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asperity_\(faults\)) just south of the city.[^cite_note-124] This fault caused the earthquakes in [1766](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1766_Istanbul_earthquake) and [1894](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1894_Istanbul_earthquake),[^cite_note-econ1214-125] and a quake of at least [magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moment_magnitude_scale) 7.0 is very likely in the 21st century,[^cite_note-126] though an earthquake with a magnitude above 7.5 is thought to be impossible.[^cite_note-127] Istanbul Municipality's Directorate of Earthquake and Ground Research is responsible for analysing the methods to reduce the [urban seismic risk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_seismic_risk),[^cite_note-128] whereas the national government-controlled [Disaster and Emergency Management Presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disaster_and_Emergency_Management_Presidency) is responsible for [earthquake emergency response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthquake_recovery), and will be helped by NGOs such as [İHH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IHH_\(Turkish_NGO\)).[^cite_note-129]

The threat of major earthquakes plays a large role in the city's infrastructure development, with over 500,000 vulnerable buildings demolished and replaced since 2012.[^cite_note-econ1214-125] According to ministry statements and geologist comments made in 2023, the city's infrastructure was in reasonably good shape, however, due to very high costs, buildings were not: over half a million flats were still vulnerable to collapse, and [casualties largely depend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthquake_casualty_estimation) on how many collapse.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132] As of 2024, most [buildings in Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buildings_in_Istanbul) were built to a low seismic standard in the 20th century,[^cite_note-133] and residents think the city is not properly [prepared for the earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthquake_preparedness).[^cite_note-134] On 23 April 2025 an earthquake with a preliminary magnitude of 6.2 shook the city and other areas. Many people were treated for injuries they suffered while trying to jump from buildings or for panic attacks.[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136] The earthquake was felt as far as provinces of [Tekirdağ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tekirda%C4%9F), [Yalova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yalova), [Bursa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bursa) and [Balıkesir](2424588.md) and in the coastal city of [İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir).[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138]

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Istanbul)



[Şile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Eile) and [Kilyos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilyos) are two seaside resorts on the Black Sea, the body of water which tempers Istanbul's climate

Istanbul's climate is [temperate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_climate); the city's position on the [Mediterranean basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_basin) and proximity to multiple bodies of water create a moderate, yet locally complicated climate with strong maritime features.[^cite_note-\:5-139] According to the [Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification), the city is considered either [hot-summer Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot-summer_Mediterranean) (*Csa*) or [humid subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical_climate) (*Cfa*),[^cite_note-140] while [Trewartha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trewartha_climate_classification) considers most of the city to be [humid subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_subtropical_climate) (*Cf*).[^cite_note-141] Alisov places the entire city into the [oceanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate) (*PmSm*) zone,[^cite_note-142] meanwhile Bohn, summarizing, describes the city's climate as one of "sub-continental-sub-Mediterranean transition".[^cite_note-143]

The city's summers are warm to hot and moderately dry, with an average daytime temperature of about 28 °C (82 °F), and less than 7 days of precipitation per month.[^cite_note-\:3-144][^cite_note-\:5-139] Despite the generally acceptable temperature range, mid-summer in Istanbul is considered moderately uncomfortable, due to high dew points and relative humidity.[^cite_note-\:3-144][^cite_note-\:5-139] Winters, meanwhile, feature daytime temperatures oscillating between 5–10 °C (41–50 °F), with frequent, sometimes intense, precipitation.[^cite_note-\:5-139]

Istanbul's precipitation is therefore unevenly distributed, with winter months getting at least twice the level of precipitation of their summerly counterparts. Cloudiness, as with precipitation, varies greatly by season. Winters are quite cloudy, with about 20 percent of days featuring sunshine. Meanwhile, summers experience 60-70 percent of possible sunshine.

&#x20;Snowfall is sporadic, but accumulates virtually every winter; and when it does, it is highly disruptive to city infrastructure. [Sea-effect snowstorms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake-effect_snow) with more than 30 centimetres (1 ft) of snowfall happen almost annually, most recently in [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Storm_Elpis).[^cite_note-\:10-145][^cite_note-146]
