#### Climate change



&#x20;[Müze Gazhane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasanpa%C5%9Fa_Gasworks) in [Kadıköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kad%C4%B1k%C3%B6y) is the first climate change museum in Turkey

Further information: [Climate change in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Turkey)

[Climate change](5042951.md) has caused an increase in Istanbul's heatwaves,[^cite_note-150] droughts,[^cite_note-151] storms,[^cite_note-152] and flooding.[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-CCTur-154] Furthermore, as Istanbul is a large and rapidly expanding city, its [urban heat island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_heat_island) has been intensifying the effects of climate change.[^cite_note-\:0-155] If trends continue, sea level rise is likely to affect city infrastructure, for example [Kadıkoy metro station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kad%C4%B1k%C3%B6y_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) is threatened with flooding.[^cite_note-156] [Xeriscaping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xeriscaping) of green spaces has been suggested,[^cite_note-157] and Istanbul has a climate-change action plan.[^cite_note-158]

### Water

This section is an excerpt from [Water supply and sanitation in Turkey § Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Turkey#Istanbul).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Turkey\&action=edit)]

[Water supply](684928.md) and [sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitation) in Istanbul is stressed by the expansion of the city.[^cite_note-159] It is the responsibility of the public utility ISKI (*Istanbul Su ve Kanalizasyon Idaresi*) created in 1981.

### Flora and fauna



Northern Istanbul as seen from the bird observatory tower in [Sarıyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sar%C4%B1yer)

The natural vegetation of the province is made up of [mixed broadleaf forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_and_broad-leaf_forests) and [pseudo-maquis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudo-maquis), reflecting the city's transitional, Mediterranean-influenced [humid temperate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_temperate_climate) climate.[^cite_note-\:4-160] [Chestnut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chestnut), [oak](39696.md), [elm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elm), [linden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tilia), [ash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraxinus) and [locust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robinia) comprise the most prominent temperate forest genera, while [laurel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurus_nobilis), [terebinth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terebinth), *[Cercis siliquastrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cercis_siliquastrum)*, [broom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spartium_junceum), [red firethorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyracantha_coccinea), and oak species such as *[Quercus cerris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quercus_cerris)* and *[Quercus coccifera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quercus_coccifera) are* the most important species of [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_forests,_woodlands,_and_scrub) and [Submediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_zones_of_the_Mediterranean_region) distribution. Apart from the natural flora, [*Platanus orentalis*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platanus_orientalis), [horse chestnut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aesculus_hippocastanum), [cypress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cupressus_sempervirens) and [stone pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_pine) make up the [introduced species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Introduced_species) that got [acclimatized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acclimatized) to Istanbul.[^cite_note-161] In a study that examined urban flora in [Kartal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartal), a total of 576 plant taxa were recorded; of those 477 were natural and 99 were exotic and cultivated. The most prominent native taxa were in the [Asteraceae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteraceae) family (50 species), while the most diverse exotic plant family was [Rosaceae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosaceae) (16 species).[^cite_note-162]

[Turkish Straits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Straits) and Sea of Marmara play a vital role for [migrating fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_migration) and other marine animals between Mediterranean, Marmara and Black Sea. Bosporus hosts [pelagic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelagic_fish), [demersal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demersal_fish) and semipelagic fish species and more than 130 different taxa have been documented in the strait.[^cite_note-163] [Bluefish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bluefish), [bonito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonito), [sea bass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_bass), [horse mackerel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse_mackerel) and [anchovies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_anchovy) compose the economically important species. Fish diversity in the waters of Istanbul has dwindled in the recent decades. From about 60 different fish species recorded in the 1970s, only 20 of them still survive in the Bosporus.[^cite_note-164] [Common bottlenose dolphin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_bottlenose_dolphin) (Turkish: *afalina*), [short-beaked common dolphin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short-beaked_common_dolphin) (Turkish: *tırtak*) and [harbor porpoise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harbour_porpoise) (Turkish: *mutur*) make up the marine mammals presently found in the Bosporus and surrounding waters, though since the 1950s the number of dolphin observations has become increasingly rare. [Mediterranean monk seals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_monk_seal) were present in Bosporus, and Princes' Islands and Tuzla shores were seal breeding areas during summer, but they have not been observed in Istanbul since the 1960s and thought to be extinct in the region.[^cite_note-165] Water pollution, [overfishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overfishing) and destruction of coastal habitats caused by urbanization are main threats to Istanbul's marine ecology.



A street cat in Istanbul

Apart from the wild land mammals Istanbul hosts a sizeable [stray animal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stray_animal) population. The presence of [feral cats in Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feral_cats_in_Istanbul) ([Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language): *sokak kedisi*) is noted to be very prevalent, with estimates ranging from a hundred thousand to over a million stray cats. The feral cats in the city have gained widespread media and public attention and are considered to be symbols of the city.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167] [Rose-ringed parakeet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose-ringed_parakeet) colonies are present in urban areas, similar to other European cities as [feral parrots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feral_parrot), and considered as [invasive species](67554.md).[^cite_note-168]

### Pollution

[Air pollution in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_pollution_in_Turkey) is acute in İstanbul with cars, buses and taxis causing frequent urban [smog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smog),[^cite_note-169] as it is one of the few European cities without a [low-emission zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-emission_zone). As of 2019 the city's mean [air quality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_quality_index) remains at a level so as to affect the heart and lungs of healthy street bystanders during peak traffic hours,[^cite_note-170] and almost 200 days of pollution were measured by the [air pollution sensors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_pollution_sensor) at [Sultangazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultangazi), [Mecidiyeköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecidiyek%C3%B6y), Alibeyköy and [Kağıthane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ka%C4%9F%C4%B1thane).[^cite_note-171] It is one of the 10 worst cities for [NO<sup></sup>\
<sub>2</sub>](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitrogen_dioxide#Long-term).[^cite_note-172] However a trial of congestion pricing is planned for the historic peninsula.[^cite_note-173]

[Algal blooms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algal_bloom) and [red tides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_tide) were reported in the Sea of Marmara and Bosporus (especially in Golden Horn), and regularly happen in urban lakes such as [Lake Büyükçekmece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_B%C3%BCy%C3%BCk%C3%A7ekmece) and [Küçükçekmece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_K%C3%BC%C3%A7%C3%BCk%C3%A7ekmece). In June 2021, a [marine mucilage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_mucilage) wave allegedly caused by water pollution spread to Sea of Marmara.[^cite_note-174]

## Cityscape

### Districts and neighborhoods

See also: [Historic Areas of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_Areas_of_Istanbul) and [List of urban centers in Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_urban_centers_in_Istanbul)

#### European side



[Fatih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatih) district comprises the [historical peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) of Istanbul

*Rumeli* ("[Rumelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumelia)") is a local nickname for the European side of Istanbul.&#x20;

The [Fatih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatih) district, which was named after Mehmed II ([Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language): *Fatih Sultan Mehmed*), corresponds to what was the whole of Constantinople until the Ottoman conquest; today it is the capital district and called the *historic peninsula* of Istanbul on the southern shore of the [Golden Horn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horn), across the medieval [Genoese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Genoa) citadel of [Galata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galata) on the northern shore. The Genoese fortifications in Galata were largely demolished in the 19th century, leaving only the [Galata Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galata_Tower), to make way for the northward expansion of the city.[^cite_note-175] Galata ([Karaköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karak%C3%B6y)) is today a quarter within the Beyoğlu district, which forms Istanbul's commercial and entertainment center and includes [İstiklal Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stiklal_Avenue) and [Taksim Square](631961.md).[^cite_note-176]

Two- and three-story colored houses with docks and balconies, built directly on the edge of the water

Originally outside the city, *[yalı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yal%C4%B1)* residences along the Bosporus are now homes in some of Istanbul's elite neighborhoods

[Dolmabahçe Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolmabah%C3%A7e_Palace), the seat of government during the late Ottoman period, is in the [Beşiktaş](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Be%C5%9Fikta%C5%9F) district on the European shore of the Bosporus, to the north of Beyoğlu. The former village of [Ortaköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ortak%C3%B6y) is within Beşiktaş and gives its name to the [Ortaköy Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ortak%C3%B6y_Mosque) on the Bosporus, near the [Bosporus Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosporus_Bridge). Lining both the European and Asian shores of the Bosporus are the historic *[yalıs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yal%C4%B1)*, luxurious chalet mansions built by Ottoman aristocrats and elites as summer homes.[^cite_note-177] Inland, north of Taksim Square is the [Istanbul Central Business District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Central_Business_District), a set of corridors lined with office buildings, residential towers, shopping centers, and university campuses, and over 2,000,000 m<sup>2</sup> (22,000,000 sq ft) of class-A office space in total. [Maslak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maslak), [Levent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levent), and [Bomonti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bomonti) are important nodes within the CBD.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOxford_Business_Group2009105-178][^cite_note-property2021-179]

The Atatürk Airport corridor is another such [edge city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edge_city)-style business, residential and shopping corridor with over 900,000 m<sup>2</sup> (9,700,000 sq ft) of class-A office space.[^cite_note-property2021-179]

#### Asian side



A view of [Levent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levent) from [Kanlıca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanl%C4%B1ca) across the Bosporus

During the Ottoman period, [Üsküdar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%9Csk%C3%BCdar) (then Scutari) and [Kadıköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kad%C4%B1k%C3%B6y) were outside the scope of the urban area, serving as tranquil outposts with seaside *yalıs* and gardens. But in the second half of the 20th century, the Asian side experienced major urban growth; the late development of this part of the city led to better infrastructure and tidier urban planning when compared with most other residential areas in the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWCTR_SocietyUnʼyu_Seisaku_Kenkyū_Kikō2004281-180] Much of the Asian side of the Bosporus functions as a suburb of the economic and commercial centers in European Istanbul, accounting for a third of the city's population but only a quarter of its employment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWCTR_SocietyUnʼyu_Seisaku_Kenkyū_Kikō2004281-180] However, [Kozyatağı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kozyata%C4%9F%C4%B1)–[Ataşehir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ata%C5%9Fehir), [Altunizade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altunizade), [Kavacık](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beykoz) and [Ümraniye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%9Cmraniye), all together having about 1.4 million sqm of class-A office space, are now important "[edge cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edge_cities)", i.e. corridors and nodes of business and shopping centers and of tall residential buildings.[^cite_note-property2021-179]

#### Expansion

As a result of Istanbul's exponential growth in the 20th century, a significant portion of the city is composed of *[gecekondus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gecekondu)* (literally "built overnight"), referring to illegally constructed squatter buildings.[^cite_note-kar7896-181] At present, some *gecekondu* areas are being gradually demolished and replaced by modern mass-housing compounds.[^cite_note-yavuz-182] Moreover, large scale [gentrification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gentrification) and [urban renewal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_renewal) projects have been taking place,[^cite_note-AtkinsonBridge2005-183] such as the one in [Tarlabaşı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarlaba%C5%9F%C4%B1);[^cite_note-184] some of these projects, like the one in [Sulukule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulukule), have faced criticism.[^cite_note-185] The Turkish government also has ambitious plans for an expansion of the city west and northwards on the European side in conjunction with the new [Istanbul Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Airport), opened in 2019; the new parts of the city will include four different settlements with specified urban functions, housing 1.5 million people.[^cite_note-186]



Panoramic view of Istanbul from the south of the [historical peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatih,_Istanbul) and above the [Sea of Marmara](28863.md). The [Golden Horn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horn) and the [Bosporus](3705.md) strait are visible in the background. Multiple business districts can also be seen, such as [Levent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levent), [Şişli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Ei%C5%9Fli) and [Maslak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maslak) on the European side (near the center of the image), and [Ataşehir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ata%C5%9Fehir) on the Asian side (at right).

### Parks



[Yıldız Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y%C4%B1ld%C4%B1z_Park) connects [Yıldız Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y%C4%B1ld%C4%B1z_Palace) to [Çırağan Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%87%C4%B1ra%C4%9Fan_Palace)

Istanbul does not have a primary urban park, but it has several green areas. [Gülhane Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%BClhane_Park) and [Yıldız Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y%C4%B1ld%C4%B1z_Park) were originally included within the grounds of two of Istanbul's palaces – Topkapı Palace and [Yıldız Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y%C4%B1ld%C4%B1z_Palace) – but they were repurposed as public parks in the early decades of the Turkish Republic.[^cite_note-187] Another park, [Fethi Paşa Korusu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fethi_Pa%C5%9Fa_Korusu), is on a hillside adjacent to the Bosphorus Bridge in Anatolia, opposite Yıldız Palace in Europe.

Along the European side, and close to the [Fatih Sultan Mehmet Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatih_Sultan_Mehmet_Bridge), is [Emirgan Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirgan_Park), which was known as the *Kyparades* ('Cypress Forest') during the Byzantine period. In the Ottoman period, it was first granted to [Nişancı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Chancellor) [Feridun Ahmed Bey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feridun_Ahmed_Bey) in the 16th century, before being granted by Sultan [Murad IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murad_IV) to the [Safavid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid_dynasty) emir Gûne Han in the 17th century, hence the name *Emirgan*. The 47-hectare (120-acre) park was later owned by Khedive [Isma'il Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isma'il_Pasha) of [Ottoman Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khedivate_of_Egypt) in the 19th century. Emirgan Park is known for its diversity of plants and an annual [tulip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulip) festival is held there since 2005.[^cite_note-188]

The [AKP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Development_Party_\(Turkey\)) government's decision to replace [Taksim Gezi Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taksim_Gezi_Park) with a replica of the Ottoman era [Taksim Military Barracks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taksim_Military_Barracks) (which was transformed into the [Taksim Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taksim_Stadium) in 1921, before being demolished in 1940 for building Gezi Park) sparked a series of [nationwide protests in 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gezi_Park_protests) covering a wide range of issues.

Popular during the summer among Istanbulites is [Belgrad Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgrad_Forest), spreading across 5,500 hectares (14,000 acres) at the northern edge of the city. The forest originally supplied water to the city and remnants of reservoirs used during Byzantine and Ottoman times survive.[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190]

### Architecture

See also: [Architecture of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Istanbul), [Ottoman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_architecture), and [Architecture of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Turkey)

A reddish building topped by a large dome and surrounded by smaller domes and four towers

The [Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia) was built by [Justinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justinian_I) in the 6th century

Istanbul is primarily known for its Byzantine and Ottoman architecture, but its buildings reflect the various peoples and empires that have previously ruled the city. Examples of Genoese and Roman architecture remain visible in Istanbul alongside their Ottoman counterparts. While nothing of the [architecture of the classical Greek period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_architecture) has survived, [Roman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Roman_architecture) has proved to be more durable. Obelisks from the [Hippodrome of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippodrome_of_Constantinople) are still visible in Sultanahmet Square, while a section of the [Valens Aqueduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valens_Aqueduct), constructed in the late 4th century, stands relatively intact at the western edge of the Fatih district.[^cite_note-coaot-191] The [Column of Constantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Column_of_Constantine), erected in 330 AD to mark the new Roman capital, still stands not far from the Hippodrome.[^cite_note-coaot-191]

Early [Byzantine architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_architecture) followed the classical Roman model of domes and arches, but improved upon these elements, as in the [Church of the Saints Sergius and Bacchus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Hagia_Sophia). The oldest surviving Byzantine church in Istanbul – albeit in ruins – is the [Monastery of Stoudios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monastery_of_Stoudios) (later converted into the Imrahor Mosque), which was built in 454.[^cite_note-192] After the recapture of Constantinople in 1261, the Byzantines enlarged two of the most important churches still extant, [Chora Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chora_Church) and [Pammakaristos Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pammakaristos_Church). Still, the pinnacle of Byzantine architecture, and one of Istanbul's most iconic structures, is the [Hagia Sophia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagia_Sophia). Topped by a dome 31 meters (102 ft) in diameter,[^cite_note-193] the Hagia Sophia stood as the world's largest cathedral for more than a thousand years, before being converted into a mosque and, as it stands now, a museum.[^cite_note-klwa171-60]



Courtyard of the [Blue Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Mosque,_Istanbul)

Among the oldest surviving examples of [Ottoman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_architecture) in Istanbul are the [Anadoluhisarı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anadoluhisar%C4%B1) and [Rumelihisarı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumelihisar%C4%B1) fortresses, which assisted the Ottomans during their siege of the city.[^cite_note-194] Over the next four centuries, the Ottomans proceeded to make an indelible impression on the skyline of Istanbul, building towering mosques and ornate palaces. The largest palace, Topkapı, includes a diverse array of architectural styles, from [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_architecture) inside the Harem, to its [Neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture) Enderûn Library.[^cite_note-195] The [imperial mosques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosques_commissioned_by_the_Ottoman_dynasty) include [Sultan Ahmed Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_Ahmed_Mosque) (the Blue Mosque), Süleymaniye Mosque, and [Yeni Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mosque_\(Istanbul\)), all of which were built at the peak of the Ottoman Empire, in the 16th and 17th centuries. In the following centuries, and especially after the *Tanzimat* reforms, Ottoman architecture was supplanted by European styles.[^cite_note-196] Areas around [İstiklal Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stiklal_Avenue) were filled with grand European embassies and rows of buildings in Neoclassical and [Renaissance Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_Revival_architecture) styles, which went on to influence the architecture of a variety of structures in Beyoğlu – including churches, stores, and theaters – and official buildings such as Dolmabahçe Palace.[^cite_note-197]

## Government and politics

### Istanbul Metropolitan Municipality



Istanbul Municipal Palace

Main article: [Istanbul Metropolitan Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Metropolitan_Municipality)

Established in 1930 and restructured with the Metropolitan Municipalities Law of 2004,[^cite_note-198] the [Istanbul Metropolitan Municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Metropolitan_Municipality) ([Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language): *İstanbul Büyükşehir Belediyesi*) is the main citywide local government authority responsible for public works, water infrastructure, transportation, cultural services, and such. It is a remarkable employer in Turkey with an accumulated amount of over 80,000 personnel.[^cite_note-199]

The current city structure traces back to the *[Tanzimat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanzimat)* reforms of the 19th century. Prior to that, local administration was held by *[Qadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qadi)*, who were appointed by the [Grand Vizier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_vizier_of_the_Ottoman_Empire). Some religious cults and [guilds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guild) also provided services for their communities.[^cite_note-200] The [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution), though Ottomans were not affected in a great extent, caused influx by foreign merchants as [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) was laid between two continents, and the imperial government signed bilateral trade agreements with many European countries, increasing the trade volume, especially with Eastern Mediterranean ports, such as [Smyrna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir), [Thessaloniki](40471.md), [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria), and [Beirut](37428.md).[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202]

In 1854, during the [Crimean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_War), intensfying ties between the Ottoman Empire and other European powers has brought the reformizing Ottoman bureaucracy into implementing local government authority for Constantinople.[^cite_note-203] The first local government authority, *Şehremaneti*, was inspired from the French [commune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communes_of_France) system.[^cite_note-204] Laws enacted after the [Ottoman constitution of 1876](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_constitution_of_1876) aimed to expand this structure across the city, imitating the twenty [arrondissements of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrondissements_of_Paris), but they were not fully implemented until 1908 when the city was declared a province with nine constituent districts.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206] Though *Şehremaneti* and its successors were disbanded by the [Turkish Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atat%C3%BCrk's_reforms), the system itself continued with the establishment of Istanbul Municipality in 1930.[^cite_note-207]

The [Municipal Council of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_Council_of_Istanbul) is the legislative organ of the Istanbul Metropolitan Municipality. Every five years, 314 local councillors are elected by popular vote. The council is the sole authority in matters regarding the municipality's jurisdiction,[^cite_note-208] with an exception, UKOME (Transportation Coordination Center), which is partnered with central government agencies.[^cite_note-209]



[Beyoğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyo%C4%9Flu) City Hall

### District municipalities

Main articles: [List of districts of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_districts_of_Istanbul) and [List of neighbourhoods of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_neighbourhoods_of_Istanbul)

District municipalities are chiefly responsible for waste management and construction projects within their respective districts, yet the law allows basically all services but [critical infrastructure](521286.md), such as public transportation and water management.[^cite_note-210] They are independent from the Istanbul Metropolitan Municipality and have their own budgets and structure with administrative autonomy to some extent. District municipalities and the metropolitan municipality often collaborate for local projects.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212]

[Muhtar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhtar_\(title\)) is the lowest-tier elected official in Turkey. Each neighborhood has a *Muhtar*, tasked with helping citizens in regards of bureaucracy and building a bridge between locals and the central government. They report to the civil administration and are the first authority to represent neighborhoods in [public policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_policy) process.[^cite_note-213]



[Istanbul Governor's Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Governor's_Office), [Fatih](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatih)

### Civil administration

Main article: [Istanbul Governor's Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Governor's_Office)

In Turkey, civil administration ([Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language): *Mülki idare*) refers to appointed officials of the [central government](3680197.md) to [provinces](428896.md) and [districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Turkey). They handle local affairs of national agencies, such as the [General Directorate of Security](20141676.md). Law enforcement in Turkey is mostly centralized, and thus Istanbul does not have a separated armed police force. [*Vali*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wali_\(administrative_title\)) and their organization are responsible for coordinating public safety efforts throughout a province.[^cite_note-214]

*[Kaymakam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaymakam)* is a representative of the central government in a district. Their nature of duties is similar to a *Vali*, yet they also handle bureaucratic works regarding the central government organizations in their respective districts. For instance, *Kaymakam* may audit a school operated by the [Ministry of National Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_National_Education_\(Turkey\)).[^cite_note-215]

### Politics

&#x20;Politically, Istanbul is seen as the most important administrative region in Turkey. In the run-up to [local elections in 2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Turkish_local_elections), Erdoğan claimed 'if we fail in Istanbul, we will fail in Turkey'.[^cite_note-216] The [contest in Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_2019_Istanbul_mayoral_election) carried deep political, economic and symbolic significance for Erdoğan, whose [election of mayor of Istanbul in 1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Istanbul_mayoral_election) had served as his launchpad.[^cite_note-inglebylove-217] For [Ekrem İmamoğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ekrem_%C4%B0mamo%C4%9Flu), winning the mayoralty of Istanbul was a huge moral victory, but for Erdoğan it had practical ramifications: His party, AKP, lost control of the \$4.8 billion municipal budget, which had sustained patronage at the point of delivery of many public services for 25 years.[^cite_note-pitel2019-218]



[Ekrem İmamoğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ekrem_%C4%B0mamo%C4%9Flu) of the [Republican People's Party](404748.md) is the 32nd and current [Mayor of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Istanbul), [first elected in 2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_2019_Istanbul_mayoral_election) and [re-elected in 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Istanbul_mayoral_election)

More recently, Istanbul and many of Turkey's metropolitan cities are following a trend away from the government and their right-wing ideology. In 2013 and 2014, large scale [anti-AKP government protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gezi_Park_protests) began in İstanbul and spread throughout the nation. This trend first became evident electorally in the [2014 mayoral election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Istanbul_mayoral_election) where the center-left opposition candidate won an impressive 40% of the vote, despite not winning. The first government defeat in Istanbul occurred in the [2017 constitutional referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Turkish_constitutional_referendum), where Istanbul voted 'No' by 51.4% to 48.6%. The AKP government had supported a 'Yes' vote and won the vote nationally due to high support in rural parts of the country. A major turning point for the government came in the 2019 local elections, where their candidate for Mayor, former Prime Minister [Binali Yıldırım](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binali_Y%C4%B1ld%C4%B1r%C4%B1m), was defeated by a very narrow margin by the [Republican People's Party](404748.md) candidate Ekrem İmamoğlu. İmamoğlu won the vote with 48.77% of the vote, against Yıldırım's 48.61%, but the elections were controversially annulled by the [Supreme Electoral Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Electoral_Council_\(Turkey\)) due to AKP's claim of electoral fraud. In the re-run İmamoğlu gathered 54.22% of the total vote and widened his margin of victory.[^cite_note-219]

Following the 2019 election, a trend towards the CHP has persisted across the city. In the [2023 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Turkish_presidential_election) the CHP candidate, [Kemal Kılıçdaroğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemal_K%C4%B1l%C4%B1%C3%A7daro%C4%9Flu), received 48.56% of the city's vote, while the incumbent president and AKP candidate, [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan), received 46.68%.

In the [2024 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Istanbul_mayoral_election), Ekrem İmamoğlu was re-elected by a 12-point margin. İmamoğlu won 51.15% of the vote, while the AKP's candidate [Murat Kurum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murat_Kurum) received 39.59%. Additionally, the CHP won the mayoralties in 26 of İstanbul's 39 districts.[^cite_note-220]

Administratively, Istanbul is divided into 39 districts, more than any other [province](428896.md) in Turkey. [Istanbul Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Province) sends 98 [Members of Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_Parliament#Turkey) to the Grand National Assembly of Turkey, which has a total of 600 seats. For the purpose of parliamentary elections, Istanbul is divided into [three electoral districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stanbul_\(electoral_districts\)); two on the European side and [one on the Asian side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stanbul_\(1st_electoral_district\)), electing 28, 35 and 35 MPs respectively.

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Istanbul)

See also: [Demographics of Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Turkey)

Throughout most of its history, Istanbul has ranked among the largest cities in the world. By 500 CE, Constantinople had somewhere between 400,000 and 500,000 people, edging out its predecessor, Rome, for the [world's largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_cities_throughout_history).[^cite_note-223] Constantinople jostled with other major historical cities, such as [Baghdad](4492.md), [Chang'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chang'an), [Kaifeng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaifeng) and [Merv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merv) for the position of the world's largest city until the 12th century. It never returned to being the world's largest, but remained the [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_European_cities_in_history) in Europe from 1500 to 1750, when it was surpassed by [London](17867.md).[^cite_note-cha463-224]

The [Turkish Statistical Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Statistical_Institute) estimates that the population of Istanbul Metropolitan Municipality was 15,519,267 at the end of 2019, hosting 19 percent of the country's population.[^cite_note-tuikpop-225] 64.4% of the residents live on the European side and 35.6% on the Asian side.[^cite_note-tuikpop-225]

Istanbul ranks as the [seventh-largest city proper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_proper_by_population) in the world, and the [second-largest urban agglomeration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_in_Europe) in Europe, after [Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_metropolitan_area).[^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227] The city's annual population growth of 1.5 percent ranks as one of the highest among the seventy-eight largest metropolises in the [Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development). The high population growth mirrors an urbanization trend across the country, as the second and third fastest-growing [OECD](33853117.md) metropolises are the Turkish cities of [İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir) and Ankara.[^cite_note-oecd-228]

Istanbul experienced especially rapid growth during the second half of the 20th century, with its population increasing tenfold between 1950 and 2000.[^cite_note-tu224-19] This growth was fueled by internal and international migration. Istanbul's foreign population with a residence permit increased dramatically, from 43,000 in 2007[^cite_note-229] to 856,377 in 2019.[^cite_note-immig-230][^cite_note-immigft-231]

According to 2020 TÜİK data, about 2.1 million people in a population of over 15.4 million have been registered[^cite_note-232] in Istanbul, meanwhile the vast majority of the residents ultimately originate from Anatolian provinces, especially those in the [Black Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea_Region), [Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Anatolia_Region) and [Eastern Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Anatolia_Region) regions due to internal migration since the 1950s.[^cite_note-233] People registered in [Kastamonu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kastamonu_Province), [Ordu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordu_Province), [Giresun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giresun_Province), [Erzurum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erzurum_Province), [Samsun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samsun_Province), [Malatya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malatya_Province), [Trabzon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trabzon_Province), [Sinop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinop_Province) and [Rize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rize_Province) provinces represent the biggest population groups in Istanbul, meanwhile people registered in [Sivas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sivas) has the highest percentage with more than 760 thousand residents in the city.[^cite_note-234] A 2019 survey found that only 36% of the Istanbul's population was born in the province.[^cite_note-\:2-235]
