| [Kurds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurds_in_Istanbul) |  | 17% |
| [Zazas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zazas) |  | 1% |
| [Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Turkey) |  | 1% |
| Others |  | 3% |



[Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greeks_in_Turkey) population in Istanbul (1844-1997), it collapses after the [Greek genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_genocide).

Istanbul has been a cosmopolitan city throughout much of its history, but it has become more homogenized since the end of the Ottoman era. The dominant ethnic group in the city is [Turkish people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_people), which also forms the majority group in Turkey. According to survey data 78% of the voting-age Turkish citizens in Istanbul state "Turkish" as their ethnic identity.[^cite_note-\:2-235]

With estimates ranging from 2 to 4 million, [Kurds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurds_in_Istanbul) form one of the largest ethnic minorities in Istanbul and are the biggest group after Turks among Turkish citizens.[^cite_note-Karadaghi1995-236][^cite_note-Tirman1997-237] According to a 2019 [KONDA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KONDA_Research_and_Consultancy) study, Kurds constituted about 17% of Istanbul's adult total population who were Turkish citizens.[^cite_note-\:2-235] Although the initial Kurdish presence in the city dates back to the early Ottoman period,[^cite_note-238] the majority of Kurds in the city originate from villages in eastern and southeastern Turkey.[^cite_note-BaserToivanen2018-239] [Zazas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zazas) are also present in the city and constitute about 1% of the total voting-age population.[^cite_note-\:2-235]

[Arabs](2185.md) form the city's other largest ethnic minority, with an estimated population of more than 2 million.[^cite_note-guardian2020-240] Following Turkey's support for the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring), Istanbul emerged as a hub for dissidents from across the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world), including former presidential candidates from Egypt, Kuwaiti MPs, and former ministers from Jordan, Saudi Arabia (including [Jamal Khashoggi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamal_Khashoggi)), Syria, and Yemen.[^cite_note-atlantic2013-241][^cite_note-nytarabs-242][^cite_note-economist2018-243] As of August 2019, the number of [refugees of the Syrian Civil War in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_the_Syrian_Civil_War_in_Turkey) residing in Istanbul was estimated to be about 1 million.[^cite_note-244] [Native Arab population in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Turkey) who are Turkish citizens are found to be making up less than 1% of city's total adult population.[^cite_note-\:2-235] As of August 2023, there were more than 530,000 refugees of the Syrian civil war in Istanbul, the highest number in any Turkish city.[^cite_note-245]



Built by [Suleiman the Magnificent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suleiman_the_Magnificent), the [Süleymaniye Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%BCleymaniye_Mosque) (1550–1557) was designed by his chief architect [Mimar Sinan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimar_Sinan), the most illustrious of all [Ottoman architects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_architecture)

[^cite_note-sinan-britannica-246]

A 2019 survey study by KONDA that examined the religiosity of the voting-age adults in Istanbul showed that 57% of the surveyed had a religion and were trying to practise its requirements. This was followed by nonobservant people with 26% who identified with a religion but generally did not practise its requirements. 11% stated they were fully devoted to their religion, meanwhile 6% were [non-believers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_Turkey) who did not believe the rules and requirements of a religion. 24% of the surveyed also identified themselves as "[religious conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_conservatives)". About 90% of Istanbul's population are [Sunni Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Muslims) and [Alevism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alevism) forms the second biggest religious group.[^cite_note-\:2-235][^cite_note-247]

Into the 19th century, the Christians of Istanbul tended to be either [Greek Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox_Church), members of the [Armenian Apostolic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Apostolic_Church) or Catholic [Levantines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levantines_\(Latin_Christians\)).[^cite_note-248] Greeks and Armenians form the largest Christian population in the city. While Istanbul's Greek population was exempted from the 1923 population exchange with [Greece](12108.md), changes in tax status and the [1955 anti-Greek pogrom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_pogrom) prompted [thousands to leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_Istanbul_Greeks).[^cite_note-greek-249] Following Greek migration to the city for work in the 2010s, the Greek population rose to nearly 3,000 in 2019, still greatly diminished since 1919, when it stood at 350,000.[^cite_note-greek-249] There are today 50,000 to 70,000 [Armenians in Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenians_in_Istanbul)[^cite_note-250] down from a peak of 164,000 in 1913.[^cite_note-251] As of 2019, an estimated 18,000 of the country's 25,000 Christian [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_people) live in Istanbul.[^cite_note-252]



There are 234 active churches and chapels in the city,

[^cite_note-heper2018-253]

&#x20;including the [Church of St. Anthony of Padua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_St._Anthony_of_Padua,_Istanbul) on [İstiklal Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stiklal_Avenue) in [Beyoğlu (Pera)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyo%C4%9Flu)

The majority of the Catholic *[Levantines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Levantine)* (Turkish: *Levanten*) in Istanbul and [İzmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0zmir) are the descendants of traders/colonists from the Italian [maritime republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_republics) of the Mediterranean (especially Genoa and [Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Venice)) and France, who obtained special rights and privileges called the [Capitulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitulations_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) from the Ottoman sultans in the 16th century.[^cite_note-254] The community had more than 15,000 members during Atatürk's presidency in the 1920s and 1930s, but today is reduced to only a few hundred, according to Italo-Levantine writer [Giovanni Scognamillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giovanni_Scognamillo).[^cite_note-255] They continue to live in Istanbul (mostly in Karaköy, Beyoğlu and [Nişantaşı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ni%C5%9Fanta%C5%9F%C4%B1)), and İzmir (mostly in [Karşıyaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kar%C5%9F%C4%B1yaka), [Bornova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bornova) and [Buca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buca)).

Istanbul became one of the world's most important Jewish centers in the 16th and 17th century.[^cite_note-epstein-256] Romaniote and Ashkenazi communities existed in Istanbul before the conquest of Istanbul, but it was the arrival of Sephardic Jews that ushered a period of cultural flourishing. Sephardic Jews settled in the city after their expulsion from Spain and Portugal in 1492 and 1497.[^cite_note-epstein-256] Sympathetic to the plight of Sephardic Jews, [Bayezid II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayezid_II) sent out the [Ottoman Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Navy) under the command of admiral [Kemal Reis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemal_Reis) to Spain in 1492 in order to evacuate them safely to Ottoman lands.[^cite_note-epstein-256] In marked contrast to [Jews in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_in_Europe), [Ottoman Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Europe) were allowed to work in any profession.[^cite_note-makovetsky-257] Ottoman Jews in Istanbul excelled in commerce and came to particularly dominate the medical profession.[^cite_note-makovetsky-257] By 1711, using the printing press, books came to be published in [Spanish](26825.md) and [Ladino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaeo-Spanish), Yiddish, and Hebrew.[^cite_note-nassigad-258] In large part due to emigration to Israel, the Jewish population in the city dropped from 100,000 in 1950[^cite_note-solomon-259] to 15,000 in 2021.[^cite_note-260][^cite_note-pop2021-261]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Istanbul)



[Istanbul Financial Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Financial_Center) in the Finanskent quarter near [Ataşehir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ata%C5%9Fehir) is the main financial district on the Asian side of the city. [Levent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levent), [Maslak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maslak) and [Şişli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Ei%C5%9Fli) are the main financial districts on the European side of Istanbul.

Istanbul had the [eleventh-largest economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_by_GDP) among the world's urban areas in 2018, and is responsible for 30 percent of Turkey's industrial output,[^cite_note-oecd2020-262] 31 percent of GDP,[^cite_note-oecd2020-262] and 47 percent of tax revenues.[^cite_note-oecd2020-262] The city's [gross domestic product](12594.md) adjusted by [PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity) stood at US\$537.507 billion in 2018,[^cite_note-263] with manufacturing and services accounting for 36 percent and 60 percent of the economic output respectively.[^cite_note-oecd2020-262] Istanbul's productivity is 110 percent higher than the national average.[^cite_note-oecd2020-262] Trade is economically important, accounting for 30 percent of the economic output in the city.[^cite_note-hisdic-123] In 2019, companies based in Istanbul produced exports worth \$83.66 billion and received imports totaling \$128.34 billion; these figures were equivalent to 47 percent and 61 percent, respectively, of the national totals.[^cite_note-wbtrade-264]

Istanbul, which straddles the Bosporus strait, houses international ports that link Europe and Asia. The Bosporus, providing the only passage from the Black Sea to the Mediterranean, is the world's busiest and narrowest [strait used for international navigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_used_for_international_navigation), with more than 200 million tons of oil passing through it each year.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOxford_Business_Group2009112-265] [International conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montreux_Convention_Regarding_the_Regime_of_the_Straits) [guarantee passage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_navigation) between the Black and the Mediterranean seas,[^cite_note-canalguardian-266] even when tankers carry oil, natural gas, chemicals, and other flammable or explosive materials as cargo. In 2011, as a workaround solution, the then Prime Minister Erdoğan presented [Canal Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_Istanbul), a project to open a new strait between the Black and Marmara seas.[^cite_note-canalguardian-266] While the project was still on Turkey's agenda in 2020, there has not been a clear date set for it.[^cite_note-hisdic-123]



[Büyükdere Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%BCy%C3%BCkdere_Avenue) in [Levent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levent)

Shipping is a significant part of the city's economy, with 73.9 percent of exports and 92.7 percent of imports in 2018 executed by sea.[^cite_note-hisdic-123] Istanbul has three major shipping ports – the [Port of Haydarpaşa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Haydarpa%C5%9Fa), the Port of Ambarlı, and the Port of Zeytinburnu – as well as several smaller ports and oil terminals along the Bosporus and the Sea of Marmara.[^cite_note-hisdic-123] Haydarpaşa, at the southeastern end of the Bosporus, was Istanbul's largest port until the early 2000s.[^cite_note-lloyds-267] Since then operations were shifted to Ambarlı, with plans to convert Haydarpaşa into a tourism complex.[^cite_note-hisdic-123] In 2019, Ambarlı, on the western edge of the urban center, had an annual capacity of 3,104,882 [TEUs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-foot_equivalent_unit), making it the third-largest cargo terminal in the Mediterranean basin.[^cite_note-lloyds-267]





[Deutsche Orientbank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche_Orientbank) (1909) in [Sirkeci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirkeci) (left) and the [Ottoman Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Bank) (1892) on [Bankalar Caddesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bankalar_Caddesi) (right)

Istanbul has been an international banking hub since the 1980s,[^cite_note-hisdic-123] and is home to the only active [stock exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exchange_\(organized_market\)) in Turkey, [Borsa Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borsa_Istanbul), which was originally established as the Ottoman Stock Exchange in 1866.[^cite_note-bnf-268] In 1995, keeping up with the financial trends, Borsa Istanbul moved its headquarters (which was originally located on [Bankalar Caddesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bankalar_Caddesi), the financial center of the Ottoman Empire,[^cite_note-bnf-268] and later at the [4th Vakıf Han](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_4th_Vak%C4%B1f_Han) building in [Sirkeci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirkeci)) to [İstinye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stinye), in the vicinity of [Maslak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maslak), which hosts the headquarters of numerous Turkish banks.[^cite_note-bist-269]

Since 2023, the [Ataşehir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ata%C5%9Fehir) district on the Asian side of the city is home to the [Istanbul Financial Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Financial_Center) (IFC), where the new headquarters of the state-owned Turkish banks, including the [Turkish Central Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bank_of_the_Republic_of_Turkey), are located.[^cite_note-ifm-aa-270][^cite_note-ifm-1-271] As of 2023, the five [tallest skyscrapers in Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_Istanbul) and Turkey are the 352 m (1,154 ft 10 in) tall [CBRT Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBRT_Tower)[^cite_note-272][^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] in the Ataşehir district on the Asian side of the city; [Metropol Istanbul Tower A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropol_Istanbul_Tower) (70 floors / 301 metres including its twin spires)[^cite_note-GAP_Insaat-275][^cite_note-aremas-276] also in the Ataşehir district; [Skyland Istanbul Towers 1 and 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skyland_%C4%B0stanbul) (2 × 284 metres)[^cite_note-277] located adjacent to [Rams Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rams_Park) in the Huzur neighbourhood of the [Sarıyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sar%C4%B1yer) district on the European side, and [Istanbul Sapphire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Sapphire) (54 floors / 238 metres; 261 metres including its spire)[^cite_note-Sapphire-278] in [Levent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levent) on the European side.

13.4 million foreign tourists visited the city in 2018, making Istanbul the world's fifth most-visited city in that year.[^cite_note-statista.com-279] Istanbul and [Antalya](361527.md) are Turkey's two largest international gateways, receiving a quarter of the nation's foreign tourists. Istanbul has more than fifty museums, with the Topkapı Palace, the most visited museum in the city, bringing in more than \$30 million in revenue each year.[^cite_note-hisdic-123] Istanbul expects 1 million tourists from cruise companies after the renovation of its cruise port, also known as [Galataport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galataport) in Karaköy district.[^cite_note-280]



A view of [Dolmabahçe Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolmabah%C3%A7e_Palace) and the skyscrapers of [Levent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levent) financial district in the background.

[^cite_note-Istanbul-panoramic1-281]

[^cite_note-levent-sporcular-park-282]

&#x20;Providing the only sea route to the Black Sea, the [Bosporus](3705.md) is the world's busiest waterway that is used for international navigation.

[^cite_note-hisdic-123]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Istanbul)



[Yalı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yal%C4%B1) houses on the [Bosporus](3705.md) are among the frequently used settings in [Turkish television drama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_television_drama)

Istanbul was historically known as a cultural hub, but its cultural scene stagnated after the Turkish Republic shifted its focus toward Ankara.[^cite_note-283] The new national government established programs that served to orient Turks toward musical traditions, especially those originating in Europe, but musical institutions and visits by foreign classical artists were primarily centered in the new capital.[^cite_note-284]

Much of Turkey's cultural scene had its roots in Istanbul, and by the 1980s and 1990s Istanbul reemerged globally as a city whose cultural significance is not solely based on its past glory.[^cite_note-285]

By the end of the 19th century, Istanbul had established itself as a regional artistic center, with Turkish, European, and Middle Eastern artists flocking to the city. Despite efforts to make Ankara Turkey's cultural heart, Istanbul had the country's primary institution of art until the 1970s.[^cite_note-gosotu2213-286] When additional universities and art journals were founded in Istanbul during the 1980s, artists formerly based in Ankara moved in.[^cite_note-287]

The façade of a masonry building, with four Greek adorning its entrance, under a clear blue sky

The [Istanbul Archaeology Museums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Archaeology_Museums), founded by [Osman Hamdi Bey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osman_Hamdi_Bey) in 1891, form Turkey's oldest modern museum

[^cite_note-kultur-288]

Beyoğlu has been transformed into the artistic center of the city, with young artists and older Turkish artists formerly residing abroad finding footing there. Modern art museums, including [İstanbul State Art and Sculpture Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stanbul_State_Art_and_Sculpture_Museum), [National Palaces Painting Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Palaces_Painting_Museum), [İstanbul Modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stanbul_Modern), the [Pera Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pera_Museum), [Sakıp Sabancı Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sak%C4%B1p_Sabanc%C4%B1_Museum), [Arter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arter_\(art_center\)) and [SantralIstanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SantralIstanbul), opened in the 2000s to complement the exhibition spaces and auction houses that have already contributed to the cosmopolitan nature of the city.[^cite_note-289] These museums have yet to attain the popularity of older museums on the historic peninsula, including the Istanbul Archaeology Museums, which ushered in the era of modern museums in Turkey, and the [Turkish and Islamic Arts Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_and_Islamic_Arts_Museum).[^cite_note-kultur-288]





[The Peninsula Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Peninsula_Hotels) (formerly the [Karaköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karak%C3%B6y) Seaport Passenger Lounge) and the restored Parcel Post Office at [Galataport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galataport), which has been renovated as a coastal recreational area with art museums, restaurants and cafés.

The first film screening in Turkey was at Yıldız Palace in 1896, a year after the technology publicly debuted in Paris.[^cite_note-290] Movie theaters rapidly cropped up in Beyoğlu, with the greatest concentration of theaters being along the street now known as İstiklal Avenue.[^cite_note-291] Istanbul also became the heart of [Turkey's nascent film industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Turkey), although Turkish films were not consistently developed until the 1950s.[^cite_note-292] Since then, Istanbul has been the most popular location to film [Turkish dramas and comedies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_television_drama).[^cite_note-293] The Turkish film industry ramped up in the second half of the century, and with *[Uzak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzak)* (2002) and *[My Father and My Son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/My_Father_and_My_Son)* (2005), both filmed in Istanbul, the nation's movies began to see substantial international success.[^cite_note-294] Istanbul and its picturesque skyline have also served as a backdrop for several foreign films, including *[From Russia with Love](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/From_Russia_with_Love_\(film\))* (1963), *[Topkapi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topkapi_\(film\))* (1964), *[The World Is Not Enough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_Is_Not_Enough)* (1999), and *[Mission Istaanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mission_Istaanbul)* (2008).[^cite_note-295]

Coinciding with this cultural reemergence was the establishment of the Istanbul Festival, which began showcasing a variety of art from Turkey and around the world in 1973. From this flagship festival came the [International Istanbul Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Istanbul_Film_Festival) and the [Istanbul Jazz Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Jazz_Festival) in the early 1980s. With its focus now solely on music and dance, the Istanbul Festival has been known as the [Istanbul International Music Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_International_Music_Festival) since 1994.[^cite_note-296] The most prominent of the festivals that evolved from the original Istanbul Festival is the [Istanbul Biennial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Biennial), held every two years since 1987. Its early incarnations were aimed at showcasing Turkish visual art, and it has since opened to international artists and risen in prestige to join the elite [biennales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biennale), alongside the [Venice Biennale](301876.md) and the [São Paulo Art Biennial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Paulo_Art_Biennial).[^cite_note-297]

### Leisure and entertainment



[İstiklal Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stiklal_Avenue) in [Beyoğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyo%C4%9Flu)

[Abdi İpekçi Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdi_%C4%B0pek%C3%A7i_Street) in [Nişantaşı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ni%C5%9Fanta%C5%9F%C4%B1), [Galataport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galataport) Shopping Area in [Karaköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karak%C3%B6y) and [Bağdat Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%C4%9Fdat_Avenue) on the Anatolian side of the city have evolved into high-end shopping districts.[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299] Other focal points for shopping, leisure and entertainment include Nişantaşı, [Ortaköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ortak%C3%B6y), [Bebek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bebek,_Be%C5%9Fikta%C5%9F) and [Kadıköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kad%C4%B1k%C3%B6y).[^cite_note-300] The city has [numerous shopping centers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_shopping_malls_in_Istanbul), from the historic to the modern. Istanbul also has an active nightlife and historic taverns, a signature characteristic of the city for centuries, if not millennia.



[Süreyya Opera House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%BCreyya_Opera_House) is situated on the Asian side of Istanbul and [Atatürk Cultural Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atat%C3%BCrk_Cultural_Center) is the main [opera house](155040.md) on the European side

The [Grand Bazaar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Bazaar,_Istanbul), in operation since 1461, is among the world's oldest and largest covered markets.[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-kos9192-302] [Mahmutpasha Bazaar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmutpasha_Bazaar,_Istanbul) is an open-air market extending between the Grand Bazaar and the [Spice Bazaar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spice_Bazaar), which has been Istanbul's major spice market since 1660.

[Galleria Ataköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galleria_Atak%C3%B6y) ushered in the age of modern shopping malls in Turkey when it opened in 1987.[^cite_note-303] Since then, malls have become major shopping centers outside the historic peninsula. [Akmerkez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akmerkez) was awarded the titles of "Europe's best" and "World's best" shopping mall by the [International Council of Shopping Centers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Council_of_Shopping_Centers) in 1995 and 1996; [Istanbul Cevahir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Cevahir) has been one of the continent's largest since opening in 2005; and [Kanyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanyon_Shopping_Mall) won the Cityscape Architectural Review Award in the Commercial Built category in 2006.[^cite_note-kos9192-302] [Zorlu Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zorlu_Center) and [İstinye Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stinye_Park) are among the other upscale [malls in Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_shopping_malls_in_Istanbul) which include the stores of the world's top fashion brands.



Abdi İpekçi Street in [Nişantaşı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ni%C5%9Fanta%C5%9F%C4%B1), one of Istanbul's premier shopping streets

Along [İstiklal Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stiklal_Avenue) is the [Çiçek Pasajı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%87i%C3%A7ek_Pasaj%C4%B1) ('Flower Passage'), a 19th-century shopping gallery which is today home to winehouses (known as *meyhanes*), pubs and restaurants.[^cite_note-304] İstiklal Avenue, originally known for its taverns, has shifted toward shopping, but the nearby Nevizade Street is still lined with winehouses and pubs.[^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306] Some other neighborhoods around İstiklal Avenue have been revamped to cater to Beyoğlu's nightlife, with formerly commercial streets now lined with pubs, cafes, and restaurants playing live music.[^cite_note-307]

Istanbul is known for its historic seafood restaurants. Many of the city's most popular and upscale seafood restaurants line the shores of the Bosporus (particularly in neighborhoods like [Ortaköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ortak%C3%B6y), [Bebek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bebek,_Be%C5%9Fikta%C5%9F), [Arnavutköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnavutk%C3%B6y), [Yeniköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yenik%C3%B6y,_Sar%C4%B1yer), [Beylerbeyi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beylerbeyi) and [Çengelköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%87engelk%C3%B6y)). [Kumkapı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumkap%C4%B1) along the Sea of Marmara has a pedestrian zone that hosts about fifty fish restaurants.[^cite_note-308]



The [Istanbul Modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Modern) Art Museum designed by [Renzo Piano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renzo_Piano)

The [Princes' Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princes'_Islands), 15 kilometers (9 mi) from the city center, are also popular for their seafood restaurants. Because of their restaurants, historic summer mansions, and tranquil, car-free streets, the Princes' Islands are a popular vacation destination among Istanbulites and foreign tourists.[^cite_note-309]

Istanbul is also famous for its sophisticated and elaborately cooked dishes of the [Ottoman cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_cuisine). Following the influx of immigrants from southeastern and eastern Turkey, which began in the 1960s, the city's foodscape has drastically changed by the end of the century; with influences of Middle Eastern cuisine such as [kebab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kebab) taking an important place in the food scene.

Restaurants featuring foreign cuisines are mainly concentrated in the [Beyoğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyo%C4%9Flu), [Beşiktaş](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Be%C5%9Fikta%C5%9F), [Şişli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Ei%C5%9Fli) and [Kadıköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kad%C4%B1k%C3%B6y) districts.

Apart from the city's numerous stadiums, sports halls and concert halls, there are several open-air venues for concerts and festivals, such as the [Cemil Topuzlu Open-Air Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cemil_Topuzlu_Open-Air_Theatre) in [Harbiye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Ei%C5%9Fli), Paraf Kuruçeşme Open-Air on the Bosphorus shore in [Kuruçeşme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuru%C3%A7e%C5%9Fme), and Parkorman in the forest of [Maslak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maslak). The annual [Istanbul Jazz Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Jazz_Festival) has been held every year since 1994. Organized between 2003 and 2013, [Rock'n Coke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock'n_Coke) was the biggest open-air rock festival in Turkey, sponsored by Coca-Cola. It was traditionally held at the [Hezarfen Airfield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezarfen_Airfield) in Istanbul.



[Zorlu Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zorlu_Center), designed by [EAA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emre_Arolat) and [Tabanlıoğlu Architects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabanl%C4%B1o%C4%9Flu_Architects), includes [Zorlu PSM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zorlu_PSM), the city's largest performing arts theatre and concert hall

The [Istanbul International Music Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_International_Music_Festival) has been held annually since 1973, and the [International Istanbul Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Istanbul_Film_Festival) has been held annually since 1982. The [Istanbul Biennial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Biennial) is a contemporary art exhibition that has been held biennially since 1987. The [Istanbul Shopping Fest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Shopping_Fest) is an annual shopping festival held since 2011, and [Teknofest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teknofest) is an annual festival of aviation, aerospace and technology, held since 2018.

When it was held for the first time in 2003, the annual [Istanbul Pride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Pride) became the first gay pride event in a Muslim-majority country.[^cite_note-310] Since 2015, all types of parades at [Taksim Square](631961.md) and [İstiklal Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stiklal_Avenue) (where, in 2013, the [Gezi Park protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gezi_Park_protests) took place) have been denied permission by the [AKP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_and_Development_Party_\(Turkey\)) government, citing security concerns, but hundreds of people have defied the ban each year. Critics have claimed that the bans were in fact due to ideological reasons.

## Sports

See also: [List of sport facilities in Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sport_facilities_in_Istanbul)









1. [Atatürk Olympic Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atat%C3%BCrk_Olympic_Stadium)

2. [Rams Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rams_Park)

3. [Şükrü Saracoğlu Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9E%C3%BCkr%C3%BC_Saraco%C4%9Flu_Stadium)

4. [Beşiktaş Stadium](40309652.md)

Istanbul is home to some of Turkey's oldest [sports clubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_club). [Beşiktaş J.K.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Be%C5%9Fikta%C5%9F_J.K.), established in 1903, is considered the oldest of these sports clubs. Due to its initial status as Turkey's only club, Beşiktaş occasionally represented the Ottoman Empire and Turkish Republic in international sports competitions, earning the right to place the Turkish flag inside its team logo.[^cite_note-fifa-bes-311] [Galatasaray S.K.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galatasaray_S.K.) and [Fenerbahçe S.K.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fenerbah%C3%A7e_S.K.) have fared better in international competitions and have won more [Süper Lig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%BCper_Lig) titles, at 24 and 19 times, respectively.[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-fifa-gal-313][^cite_note-314] Galatasaray and Fenerbahçe have a long-standing rivalry, with Galatasaray based in the European part and Fenerbahçe based in the Anatolian part of the city.[^cite_note-fifa-gal-313] Istanbul has seven basketball teams – [Anadolu Efes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anadolu_Efes_S.K.), [Beşiktaş](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Be%C5%9Fikta%C5%9F_J.K._\(men's_basketball\)), [Darüşşafaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dar%C3%BC%C5%9F%C5%9Fafaka_S.K.), [Fenerbahçe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fenerbah%C3%A7e_Men's_Basketball), [Galatasaray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galatasaray_S.K._\(men's_basketball\)), [İstanbul Büyükşehir Belediyespor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stanbul_B%C3%BCy%C3%BCk%C5%9Fehir_Belediyespor_\(basketball\)) and [Büyükçekmece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%BCy%C3%BCk%C3%A7ekmece_Basketbol) – that play in the premier-level [Basketbol Süper Ligi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basketbol_S%C3%BCper_Ligi).[^cite_note-315]

Many of Istanbul's sports facilities have been built or upgraded since 2000 to bolster the city's bids for the [Summer Olympic Games](27566.md). [Atatürk Olympic Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atat%C3%BCrk_Olympic_Stadium), the largest multi-purpose stadium in Turkey, was completed in 2002 as an [IAAF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Association_of_Athletics_Federations) first-class venue for [track and field](194904.md).[^cite_note-316] The stadium hosted the [2005 UEFA Champions League Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_UEFA_Champions_League_Final), and was selected by the [UEFA](32332.md) to host the CL Final games of [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_UEFA_Champions_League_Final) and [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_UEFA_Champions_League_Final), which were relocated to [Lisbon](18091.md) (2020) and [Porto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto) (2021) due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md).[^cite_note-317] [Şükrü Saracoğlu Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9E%C3%BCkr%C3%BC_Saraco%C4%9Flu_Stadium), Fenerbahçe's home field, hosted the [2009 UEFA Cup Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_UEFA_Cup_Final) three years after its completion. [Türk Telekom Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%BCrk_Telekom_Arena) opened in 2011 to replace [Ali Sami Yen Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Sami_Yen_Stadium) as Galatasaray's home turf,[^cite_note-318][^cite_note-319] while [Beşiktaş Stadium](40309652.md), opened in 2016 to replace [BJK İnönü Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BJK_%C4%B0n%C3%B6n%C3%BC_Stadium) as the home turf of Beşiktaş, hosted the [2019 UEFA Super Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_UEFA_Super_Cup) game. All four stadiums are elite [Category 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_stadium_categories) (formerly five-star) UEFA stadiums.[^cite_note-uefa-category-323]

The [Sinan Erdem Dome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinan_Erdem_Dome), among the largest indoor arenas in Europe, hosted the final of the [2010 FIBA World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_FIBA_World_Championship), the [2012 IAAF World Indoor Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_IAAF_World_Indoor_Championships), as well as the [2011–12 Euroleague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011%E2%80%9312_Euroleague) and [2016–17 EuroLeague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016%E2%80%9317_EuroLeague) Final Fours.[^cite_note-turkey2010-324] Prior to the completion of the Sinan Erdem Dome in 2010, [Abdi İpekçi Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdi_%C4%B0pek%C3%A7i_Arena) was Istanbul's primary indoor arena, having hosted the finals of [EuroBasket 2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroBasket_2001).[^cite_note-turkey2010-324] Several other indoor arenas, including the [Beşiktaş Akatlar Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BJK_Akatlar_Arena), have also been inaugurated since 2000, serving as the home courts of Istanbul's sports clubs. The most recent of these is the 13,800-seat [Ülker Sports Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%9Clker_Sports_Arena), which opened in 2012 as the home court of Fenerbahçe's basketball teams.[^cite_note-325] Despite the construction boom, five bids for the Summer Olympics – in [2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Summer_Olympics), [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Summer_Olympics), [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Summer_Olympics), [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Summer_Olympics), and [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Summer_Olympics) – and national bids for [UEFA Euro 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2012) and [UEFA Euro 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2016) have ended unsuccessfully.[^cite_note-326] The city will host the [2027 edition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2027_European_Games) of the [European Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Games).[^cite_note-327]

The [TVF Burhan Felek Sport Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TVF_Burhan_Felek_Sport_Hall) is one of the major volleyball arenas in the city and hosts clubs[^cite_note-328] such as [Eczacıbaşı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eczac%C4%B1ba%C5%9F%C4%B1_Volleyball),[^cite_note-329] [Vakıfbank SK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vak%C4%B1fBank_S.K.), and [Fenerbahçe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fenerbah%C3%A7e_S.K._\(women's_volleyball\)) who have won numerous [European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CEV_Women's_Champions_League) and [World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIVB_Women's_Volleyball_Club_World_Championship) titles.

Between the 2005–2011 seasons,[^cite_note-330] and in the 2020 season,[^cite_note-331] [Istanbul Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Park) racing circuit hosted the Formula One [Turkish Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Grand_Prix). The 2021 F1 Turkish Grand Prix was initially cancelled due to the COVID-19 pandemic,[^cite_note-332] but on 25 June 2021, it was announced that the 2021 F1 Turkish Grand Prix will take place on 3 October 2021.[^cite_note-333] Istanbul Park was also a venue of the [World Touring Car Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Touring_Car_Championship) and the [European Le Mans Series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Le_Mans_Series) in 2005 and 2006, but the track has not seen either of these competitions since then.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335] It also hosted the [Turkish Motorcycle Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Motorcycle_Grand_Prix) between 2005 and 2007. Istanbul was occasionally a venue of the [F1 Powerboat World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F1_Powerboat_World_Championship), with the last race on the Bosporus strait on 12–13 August 2000.[^cite_note-336] The last race of the [Powerboat P1 World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offshore_powerboat_racing#UIM_Powerboat_GPS_World_Championship) on the Bosporus took place on 19–21 June 2009.[^cite_note-337] Istanbul Sailing Club, established in 1952, hosts races and other sailing events on the waterways in and around Istanbul each year.[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339]

## Media



[Küçük Çamlıca TV Radio Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%BC%C3%A7%C3%BCk_%C3%87aml%C4%B1ca_TV_Radio_Tower) (369 m (1,211 ft)) is the tallest structure in the city

[^cite_note-340]

Most state-run radio and television stations are based in Ankara, but Istanbul is the primary hub of Turkish media. The industry has its roots in the former Ottoman capital, where the first Turkish newspaper, *[Takvim-i Vekayi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takvim-i_Vekayi)* (Calendar of Affairs), was published in 1831. The [Cağaloğlu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ca%C4%9Falo%C4%9Flu) street on which the newspaper was printed, Bâb-ı Âli Street, rapidly became the center of Turkish print media, alongside Beyoğlu across the Golden Horn.[^cite_note-341]

&#x20;Istanbul now has a wide variety of periodicals. Most nationwide newspapers are based in Istanbul, with simultaneous Ankara and İzmir editions.[^cite_note-loc-pro-342] *[Hürriyet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%BCrriyet)*, *[Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabah_\(newspaper\))*, *[Posta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posta_\(newspaper\))* and *[Sözcü](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%B6zc%C3%BC)*, the country's top four papers, are all headquartered in Istanbul, boasting more than 275,000 weekly sales each.[^cite_note-343] *Hürriyet*'s English-language edition, *[Hürriyet Daily News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%BCrriyet_Daily_News)*, has been printed since 1961, but the English-language *[Daily Sabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daily_Sabah)*, first published by *Sabah* in 2014, has overtaken it in circulation. Several smaller newspapers, including popular publications like *[Cumhuriyet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumhuriyet)*, *[Milliyet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milliyet)* and *[Habertürk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habert%C3%BCrk)* are also based in Istanbul.[^cite_note-loc-pro-342] Istanbul also has long-running [Armenian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language) newspapers, notably the dailies *[Marmara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marmara_\(newspaper\))* and *[Jamanak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamanak)* and the bilingual weekly *[Agos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agos)* in Armenian and Turkish.[^cite_note-344]



Istanbul Radio House

Office in Eminönü. Control of this transmission, and other radio stations established in the following decades, ultimately came under the state-run [Turkish Radio and Television Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Radio_and_Television_Corporation) (TRT), which held a monopoly on radio and television broadcasts between its founding in 1964 and 1990.[^cite_note-trt-radio-345] Today, TRT runs four national radio stations; these stations have transmitters across the country so each can reach over 90 percent of the country's population, but only Radio 2 is based in Istanbul. Offering a range of content from educational programming to coverage of sporting events, Radio 2 is the most popular radio station in Turkey.[^cite_note-trt-radio-345] Istanbul's airwaves are the busiest in Turkey, primarily featuring either Turkish-language or English-language content. One of the exceptions, offering both, is [Açık Radyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A%C3%A7%C4%B1k_Radyo) (94.9 FM). Among Turkey's first private stations, and the first featuring foreign popular music, was Istanbul's [Metro FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_FM_\(Turkey\)) (97.2 FM). The state-run Radio 3, although based in Ankara, also features English-language popular music, and English-language news programming is provided on NTV Radyo (102.8 FM).[^cite_note-346]

TRT-Children is the only TRT television station based in Istanbul.[^cite_note-347] Istanbul is home to the headquarters of several Turkish stations and regional headquarters of international media outlets. Istanbul-based [Star TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_TV_\(Turkey\)) was the first private television network to be established following the end of the TRT monopoly; Star TV and [Show TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Show_TV) (also based in Istanbul) remain highly popular throughout the country, airing Turkish and American series.[^cite_note-348] [Kanal D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanal_D) and [ATV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atv_\(Turkey\)) are other stations in Istanbul that offer a mix of news and series; [NTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NTV_\(Turkey\)) and [360](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/360_\(TV_channel\)) – both based in the city – are mainly just known for their news coverage in Turkish. The [BBC](19344654.md) has a regional office in Istanbul, assisting its Turkish-language news operations. The American news channel [CNN](62028.md) licensed its brand to the Turkish-language [CNN Türk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNN_T%C3%BCrk) in 1999.[^cite_note-349]

## Education

Further information: [Education in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Turkey)

A triumphal arch adjacent to a Turkish flag and in front of an open plaza

Main entrance gate of [Istanbul University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_University), the city's oldest Turkish institution, established in 1453

As of 2019, excluding universities more than 3.1 million students attended [7,437 schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_schools_in_Istanbul) in Istanbul, about half of the schools being [private schools](612857.md).[^cite_note-\:1-350] The average class size was 30 for primary education institutes, 27 for vocational schools and 23 for general high schools.[^cite_note-\:1-350] Of the 842 public high schools, 263 are [vocational schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocational_school), another 263 are [Anatolian high schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_High_School), 207 are religiously oriented [İmam Hatip schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0mam_Hatip_school), and 14 are [STEM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/STEM)-oriented [science high schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_education).[^cite_note-351] [Galatasaray High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galatasaray_High_School) was established in 1481 and is the oldest public high school in Turkey.[^cite_note-educationheper-352] [Kabataş Erkek Lisesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabata%C5%9F_Erkek_Lisesi), [Istanbul Lisesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Lisesi) and [Cağaloğlu Anatolian High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ca%C4%9Falo%C4%9Flu_Anatolian_High_School) are among other public high schools in the city. Istanbul also contains high schools established by the European and American expatriates and missionaries in the 19th century that currently offer secular, foreign-language education such as [Robert College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_College), [Deutsche Schule Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche_Schule_Istanbul), [Sankt Georgs-Kolleg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._George's_Austrian_High_School), [Lycée Saint-Joseph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyc%C3%A9e_Saint-Joseph,_Istanbul) and [Liceo Italiano di Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liceo_Italiano_di_Istanbul).[^cite_note-353] Furthermore [Turkish citizens of Jewish, Armenian, Greek and Assyrian descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minorities_in_Turkey) are allowed to establish and attend their respective schools as granted in the [Treaty of Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Lausanne), [Phanar Greek Orthodox College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phanar_Greek_Orthodox_College) being an example.[^cite_note-354] Most [high schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Turkey#Secondary_education) are highly selective and demand high scores from the national standardized [LGS exam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGS_exam?action=edit\&redlink=1) for admission, with Galatasaray and Robert College only accepting the top 0.1% to 0.01% of the exam takers.[^cite_note-355]

Istanbul contains almost a third of all [universities in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Turkey). As of 2019 Istanbul has 61 colleges and universities, with more than 1.8 million students enrolled according to official figures. Of those, fourteen are state-owned, 44 are "foundation-owned" [private universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_universities) and three are foundation-owned [vocational universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocational_universities) of higher education. There are also military academies, including the [Turkish Air Force Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Air_Force_Academy) and [Turkish Naval Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Naval_Academy) as well as four foundation-owned vocational universities of higher education which are not affiliated with any university.[^cite_note-356]





[Maçka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma%C3%A7ka,_Istanbul) (left) and Taşkışla (right) campuses of [Istanbul Technical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Technical_University)

Some of the most renowned and highly ranked universities in Turkey are in Istanbul. [Istanbul University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_University), the nation's oldest institute of higher education, dates back to 1453 and its dental, law, medical schools were founded in the 19th century. The city's largest private universities include [Sabancı University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabanc%C4%B1_University), with its main campus in [Tuzla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuzla,_Istanbul), [Koç University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ko%C3%A7_University) in Sarıyer, [Özyeğin Üniversitesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%96zye%C4%9Fin_%C3%9Cniversitesi) near [Altunizade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altunizade). Istanbul's first private university, Koç University, was founded as late as 1992, because private universities were not allowed in Turkey before the [1982 amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Turkey) to the constitution.[^cite_note-educationheper-352] Istanbul is also home to several conservatories and art schools, including [Mimar Sinan Academy of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimar_Sinan_Fine_Arts_University), founded in 1882.[^cite_note-finearts-357]

Public universities with a major presence in the city, such as Istanbul University, [Istanbul Technical University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Technical_University) (the world's third-oldest university dedicated entirely to engineering, established in 1773), and [Boğaziçi University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bo%C4%9Fazi%C3%A7i_University) (formerly the higher education section of Robert College until 1971) provide education in English as the primary foreign language, while the primary foreign language of education at [Galatasaray University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galatasaray_University) is French.[^cite_note-educationheper-352]

## Public services

Main article: [Utilities in Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utilities_in_Istanbul)

Further information: [Telecommunications in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_in_Turkey) and [Health care in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_in_Turkey)

A brick factory stands in front of a park, with open green space, a reflecting pool, and benches

The [Silahtarağa Power Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silahtara%C4%9Fa_Power_Station), known as [SantralIstanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SantralIstanbul), part of the [Istanbul Bilgi University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Bilgi_University), was Istanbul's sole source of power between 1914 and 1952

Istanbul's first [water supply](684928.md) systems date back to the city's early history, when [aqueducts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqueduct_\(watercourse\)) (such as the [Valens Aqueduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valens_Aqueduct)) deposited the water in the city's numerous [cisterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cistern).[^cite_note-iskitarihce-358] At the behest of Suleiman the Magnificent, the Kırkçeşme water supply network was constructed; by 1563, the network provided 4,200 cubic meters (150,000 ft<sup>3</sup>) of water to 158 sites each day.[^cite_note-iskitarihce-358] In later years, in response to increasing public demand, water from various springs was channeled to public fountains, like the [Fountain of Ahmed III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fountain_of_Ahmed_III), by means of supply lines.[^cite_note-359] Today, Istanbul has a chlorinated and filtered water supply and a [sewage treatment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sewage_treatment) system managed by the Istanbul Water and Sewerage Administration (İstanbul Su ve Kanalizasyon İdaresi, İSKİ).[^cite_note-360]

The [Silahtarağa Power Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silahtara%C4%9Fa_Power_Station), a [coal-fired power station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal-fired_power_station) along the Golden Horn, was the sole source of Istanbul's electricity between 1914, when its first engine room was completed, and 1952.[^cite_note-silah-361] Following the founding of the Turkish Republic, the plant underwent renovations to accommodate the city's increasing demand; its capacity grew from 23 megawatts in 1923 to a peak of 120 megawatts in 1956.[^cite_note-silah-361][^cite_note-teias-362] Capacity declined until the power station reached the end of its economic life and shut down in 1983.[^cite_note-silah-361] The state-run Turkish Electrical Authority (TEK) briefly – between its founding in 1970 and 1984 – held a monopoly on the generation and distribution of electricity, but now the authority – since split between the Turkish Electricity Generation Transmission Company (TEAŞ) and the Turkish Electricity Distribution Company (TEDAŞ) – competes with private [electric utilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_utilities).[^cite_note-teias-362]

An arched neoclassical building with hanging PTT banners

The [Grand Post Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Main_Post_Office) in [Sirkeci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirkeci), Istanbul, was designed by [Vedat Tek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedat_Tek) in the [Turkish neoclassical style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Turkey#1920s_to_early_1930s:_First_national_architectural_movement) of the early 20th century

[^cite_note-363]

The Ottoman Ministry of Post and Telegraph was established in 1840 and the first post office, the Imperial Post Office, opened near the courtyard of Yeni Mosque. By 1876, the first international mailing network between Istanbul and the lands beyond the Ottoman Empire had been established.[^cite_note-PTT-364] Sultan [Abdülmecid I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd%C3%BClmecid_I) issued [Samuel Morse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Morse) his first official honor for the [electrical telegraph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrical_telegraph) in 1847, and construction of the first telegraph line – between Istanbul and [Edirne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edirne) – finished in time to announce the end of the [Crimean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_War) in 1856.[^cite_note-365]

A nascent telephone system began to emerge in Istanbul in 1881 and after the first manual [telephone exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telephone_exchange) became operational in Istanbul in 1909, the Ministry of Post and Telegraph became the Ministry of Post, Telegraph, and Telephone.[^cite_note-PTT-364][^cite_note-366] [GSM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GSM) cellular networks arrived in Turkey in 1994, with Istanbul among the first cities to receive the service.[^cite_note-turkt-367] Today, [mobile](19644137.md) and [landline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landline) service is provided by private companies, after [Türk Telekom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%BCrk_Telekom), which split from the Ministry of Post, Telegraph, and Telephone in 1995, was privatized in 2005.[^cite_note-PTT-364][^cite_note-turkt-367] Postal services remain under the purview of what is now the Post and Telegraph Organization (retaining the acronym PTT).[^cite_note-PTT-364]



Modern [Başakşehir Çam and Sakura City Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%C5%9Fak%C5%9Fehir_%C3%87am_and_Sakura_City_Hospital) is a large district general hospital in Başakşehir district of Istanbul.

In 2000, Istanbul had 137 hospitals, of which 100 were private.[^cite_note-368] Turkish citizens are entitled to subsidized healthcare in the nation's state-run hospitals.[^cite_note-loc-pro-342] As public hospitals tend to be overcrowded or otherwise slow, private hospitals are preferable for those who can afford them. Their prevalence has increased significantly over the last decade, as the percentage of outpatients using private hospitals increased from 6 percent to 23 percent between 2005 and 2009.[^cite_note-loc-pro-342][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOxford_Business_Group2009197-369] Many of these private hospitals, as well as some of the public hospitals, are equipped with high-tech equipment, including [MRI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetic_resonance_imaging) machines, or associated with medical research centers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOxford_Business_Group2009198-370] Turkey has more hospitals accredited by the United States–based [Joint Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Commission) than any other country in the world, with most concentrated in its big cities. The high quality of healthcare, especially in private hospitals, has contributed to a recent upsurge in [medical tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_tourism) to Turkey (with a 40 percent increase between 2007 and 2008).[^cite_note-371] Laser [eye surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eye_surgery) and [hair transplant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hair_transplant) surgery is particularly common among medical tourists, as Turkey is known for specializing in the procedure.[^cite_note-372]

## Transportation

The [suspension bridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suspension_bridge) at the Bosporus



[15 July Martyrs Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosphorus_Bridge)



[Fatih Sultan Mehmet Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatih_Sultan_Mehmet_Bridge)



[Yavuz Sultan Selim Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yavuz_Sultan_Selim_Bridge)

Main articles: [Public transport in Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transport_in_Istanbul), [Istanbul Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Metro), and [Ferries in Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferries_in_Istanbul)

Further information: [Roads in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roads_in_Turkey), [Rail transport in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Turkey), and [Aviation in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aviation_in_Turkey)

### Roads

Istanbul's motorways network are the [O-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otoyol_1), [O-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otoyol_2), [O-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otoyol_3), [O-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otoyol_4) and [O-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otoyol_7). The total length of Istanbul Province's network of [toll roads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toll_road) is 543 km (337 mi) (2024) and the state highways network (*devlet yolları*) is 353 km (219 mi) (2024), totaling 896 km (557 mi) of expressway roads (minimum 2x2 lanes), excluding secondary roads and urban streets.[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374][^cite_note-Otoyollar1-375] The density of expressway network is 16.8 km/100 km<sup>2</sup>. The O-1 forms the city's inner [ring road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ring_road), traversing the [Bosphorus Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosphorus_Bridge), and the O-2 is the city's outer ring road, crossing the [Fatih Sultan Mehmet Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatih_Sultan_Mehmet_Bridge). The O-2 continues west to Edirne and the O-4 continues east to Ankara. The O-2, O-3, and O-4 are part of [European route E80](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_route_E80) (the Trans-European Motorway) between Portugal and the [Iran–Turkey border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Turkey_border).[^cite_note-googma-376] In 2011, the first and second bridges on the Bosphorus carried 400,000 vehicles each day.[^cite_note-377] The O-7[^cite_note-Otoyollar1-375] or Kuzey Marmara Otoyolu, is a motorway that bypass Istanbul to the north. The O-7 motorway from Kinali Gişeleri to Istanbul Park Service has 139.2 km (86.5 mi), with 8 lanes (4x4), and from Odayeri-K10 to [Istanbul Atatürk Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Atat%C3%BCrk_Airport) has 30.4 km (18.9 mi).[^cite_note-Otoyollar1-375] The completed section of highway crosses the Bosporus via the [Yavuz Sultan Selim (Third Bosphorus) Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yavuz_Sultan_Selim_Bridge), entered service on 26 August 2016.[^cite_note-378] The O-7 (Northern Beltway) 3.Beltway connects Istanbul Atatürk Airport with Istanbul Airport and [Sabiha Gökçen Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabiha_G%C3%B6k%C3%A7en_Airport).[^cite_note-379] Environmentalist groups worry that the third bridge will endanger the remaining green areas to the north of Istanbul.[^cite_note-eib-380][^cite_note-let-381] Apart from the three Bosphorus Bridges, the dual-deck, 14.6-kilometer (9.1 mi) [Eurasia Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia_Tunnel) (which entered service on 20 December 2016) under the Bosphorus strait also provides road crossings for motor vehicles between the Asian and European sides of Turkey.[^cite_note-382] Road transport emits significant carbon dioxide, estimated at 7 million tons in 2021.[^cite_note-383]

### Public transportation



The [Tünel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%BCnel) was inaugurated on January 17, 1875, making it the second-oldest underground [urban railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit) in the world after the [London Underground](17839.md) which opened on January 10, 1863.

[^cite_note-Tunel-384]

&#x20;It was originally powered by two 150 [HP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horsepower) [steam-power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steam_engine) plants.

[^cite_note-Tunel-384]

&#x20;Wagons were lit up with [gas lamps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas_lamp) due to the lack of [electric power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_power) in that period.

[^cite_note-Tunel-384]

Istanbul's local public transportation system is a network of [commuter rail](6286.md), [trams](30733.md), [funiculars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funicular), [metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_\(rapid_transit\)) lines, buses, [bus rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_rapid_transit), and [ferries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferries_in_Istanbul). Fares across modes are integrated, using the [contactless](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contactless_smart_card) [Istanbulkart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbulkart), introduced in 2009, or the older [Akbil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbil_\(smart_ticket\)) electronic ticketing device.[^cite_note-385] Trams in Istanbul [date back to 1872](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trams_in_Istanbul_\(1871-1966\)), when they were horse-drawn, but even the first electrified trams were decommissioned in the 1960s,[^cite_note-iett-history-386] replaced by a [trolleybus system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trolleybuses_in_Istanbul) which operated until the 1980s.[^cite_note-murray-387][^cite_note-albe-montanarini-388] Operated by [Istanbul Electricity, Tramway and Tunnels General Management (İETT)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Electricity,_Tram_and_Tunnel_Establishments), trams slowly returned to the city in the 1990s with the introduction of the [Istanbul nostalgic tram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_nostalgic_tram) and a faster [modern tram line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_modern_tram), which now carries 265,000 passengers each day.[^cite_note-iett-history-386][^cite_note-389] The [Tünel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%BCnel) opened in 1875 as the world's second-oldest subterranean rail line, after the [Metropolitan Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Railway) in London.[^cite_note-Tunel-384][^cite_note-iett-history-386] It still carries passengers between Karaköy and İstiklal Avenue along a steep 573-meter (1,880 ft) track; a more modern funicular between Taksim Square and [Kabataş](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabata%C5%9F,_Istanbul) began running in 2006.[^cite_note-390][^cite_note-391]

The [Istanbul Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Metro) comprises ten lines (the [M1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M1_\(Istanbul\)), [M2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M2_\(Istanbul_Metro\)), [M3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M3_\(Istanbul_Metro\)), [M6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M6_\(Istanbul_Metro\)), [M7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M7_\(Istanbul_Metro\)), [M9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M9_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) and [M11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M11_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) on the European side, and the [M4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M4_\(Istanbul_Metro\)), [M5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M5_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) and [M8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M8_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) on the Asian side) with several other lines ([M12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M12_\(Istanbul_Metro\)) and [M14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M14_\(Istanbul_Metro\))) and extensions under construction.[^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393] The two sides of Istanbul's metro are connected under the Bosphorus by the [Marmaray Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marmaray_Tunnel), inaugurated in 2013 as the first rail connection between Thrace and Anatolia, having 13.5 km (8.4 mi) length.[^cite_note-394] The Marmaray tunnel together with the suburban railways lines along the Sea of Marmara, form the intercontinental commuter rail line in Istanbul, named officially B1, from [Halkalı](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halkal%C4%B1_railway_station) on the European side to [Gebze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gebze) on the Asian side. This rail line has 76.6 km (47.6 mi), and the full line opened on 12 March 2019.[^cite_note-395] Until then, buses provide transportation within and between the two-halves of the city, accommodating 2.2 million passenger trips each day.[^cite_note-396] The [Metrobus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metrobus_\(Istanbul\)), a form of bus rapid transit, crosses the Bosphorus Bridge, with dedicated lanes leading to its termini.[^cite_note-397]

- A nostalgic tram on İstiklal Avenue, decorated for the 112th anniversary of the city's electric powered tram service which began in 1914, replacing the horse-drawn tram service between 1872 and 1914

  [^cite_note-398]

### Ferries



M/V *Emin Kul*, a *[vapur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferries_in_Istanbul)* operated by [Şehir Hatları](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Eehir_Hatlar%C4%B1), crossing the Bosphorus



An [İDO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0DO) *deniz otobüsü* (seabus) high-speed ferry departs Istanbul

There are three main [ferry operators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferries_in_Istanbul) in Istanbul. The municipally owned [Şehir Hatları](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Eehir_Hatlar%C4%B1) operates the traditional *vapur* ferries on 891 daily trips between 53 piers across the Bosporus and the [Princes' Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princes'_Islands).[^cite_note-399]

The privately owned [İDO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0DO) (Istanbul Sea Buses) runs a combination of high-speed passenger ferries and vehicle ferries within Istanbul and to destinations across the Sea of Marmara.[^cite_note-400] A smaller private company, [Turyol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turyol) also operates services across the Bosphorus. The city's main cruise ship terminal is the [Port of Istanbul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Istanbul) in Karaköy, with a capacity of 10,000 passengers per hour.[^cite_note-401]

### Railroads



Originally opened in 1873 with a smaller terminal building as the main terminus at the Ottoman [Chemins de fer Orientaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemins_de_fer_Orientaux), which connected Istanbul with [Vienna](55866.md), the current [Sirkeci Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stanbul_Sirkeci_Terminal) building was constructed between 1888 and 1890, and became the eastern terminus of the [Orient Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orient_Express) from [Paris](22989.md).

[^cite_note-402]

International rail service from Istanbul launched in 1889, with a line between Bucharest and Istanbul's [Sirkeci Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirkeci_Terminal), which ultimately became famous as the eastern terminus of the [Orient Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orient_Express) from Paris.[^cite_note-ha251-88] Regular service to Bucharest and [Thessaloniki](40471.md) continued until the early 2010s, when the former was interrupted for Marmaray construction but started running again in 2019 and the latter was halted due to economic problems in Greece.[^cite_note-403][^cite_note-404] After Istanbul's [Haydarpaşa Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haydarpa%C5%9Fa_Terminal) opened in 1908, it served as the western terminus of the [Baghdad Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baghdad_Railway) and an extension of the [Hejaz Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hejaz_Railway); today, neither service is offered directly from Istanbul.[^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407] Service to Ankara and other points across Turkey is normally offered by [Turkish State Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_State_Railways), but the construction of Marmaray and the [Ankara-Istanbul high-speed railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ankara-Istanbul_high-speed_railway) forced the station to close in 2012.[^cite_note-akay-408] New stations to replace both the Haydarpaşa and Sirkeci terminals, and connect the city's disjointed railway networks, now the Marmaray second phase opened to the public.[^cite_note-akay-408] Private bus companies still operation to this day. Istanbul's main bus station is the largest in Europe, with a daily capacity of 15,000 buses and 600,000 passengers, serving destinations as distant as [Frankfurt](10992.md).[^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410]

### Airports

Istanbul has had three large international airports, two of which currently serve commercial passenger flights. The largest is the new [Istanbul Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istanbul_Airport), opened in 2018 in the [Arnavutköy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnavutk%C3%B6y_\(district\)) district to the northwest of the city center, on the European side, near the Black Sea coast.

All scheduled commercial passenger flights were transferred from [Atatürk Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atat%C3%BCrk_Airport) to Istanbul Airport on 6 April 2019, following the closure of Istanbul Atatürk Airport for scheduled passenger flights.[^cite_note-411] The [IATA code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IATA_code) IST was also transferred to the new airport.[^cite_note-412] Once all phases are completed in 2025, the airport will have six sets of runways (eight in total), 16 taxiways, and will be able to accommodate 200 million passengers a year.[^cite_note-413][^cite_note-414] The transfer from the airport to the city is via the O-7, and it will eventually be linked by two lines of the Istanbul Metro.

[Sabiha Gökçen International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabiha_G%C3%B6k%C3%A7en_International_Airport), 45 kilometers (28 mi) southeast of the city center, on the Asian side, was opened in 2001 to relieve Atatürk. Dominated by [low-cost carriers](428422.md), Istanbul's second airport has rapidly become popular, especially since the opening of a new international terminal in 2009;[^cite_note-415] the airport handled 14.7 million passengers in 2012, a year after [Airports Council International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airports_Council_International) named it the world's fastest-growing airport.[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417] Atatürk had also experienced rapid growth, as its 20.6 percent rise in passenger traffic between 2011 and 2012 was the highest among the world's top 30 airports.[^cite_note-aci-418]

Istanbul Atatürk Airport, located 24 kilometers (15 mi) west of the city center, on the European side, near the Marmara Sea coast, was formerly the city's largest airport. After its closure to commercial flights in 2019, it was briefly used by cargo aircraft and the official state aircraft owned by the Turkish government, until the demolition of its runway began in 2020. It handled 61.3 million passengers in 2015, which made it the [third-busiest airport in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_busiest_airports_in_Europe) and the [18th-busiest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World's_busiest_airports_by_passenger_traffic) in that year.[^cite_note-aci-418]

[^cite_note-11]: &#x20;Istanbul, while the largest in Europe if the entire city is counted as European, straddles both Europe and Asia, with its commercial and historical centre and two-thirds of the population in Europe, the rest in Asia. Since Istanbul is a transcontinental city, [Moscow](19004.md) is the largest city entirely within Europe.

[^cite_note-byz-date-49]: &#x20;The foundation of Byzantion ([Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantium)) is sometimes, especially in encyclopedic or other tertiary sources, placed firmly in 667 BC. Historians have disputed the precise year the city was founded. Commonly cited is the work of 5th-century-BC historian [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus), which says the city was founded seventeen years after [Chalcedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcedon),<sup>[\[41\]](#cite_note-44)</sup> which came into existence around 685 BC. [Eusebius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eusebius) concurs with 685 BC as the year Chalcedon was founded, but places Byzantion's establishment in 659 BC.<sup>[\[42\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsaac1986199-45)</sup> Among more modern historians, Carl Roebuck proposed the 640s BC<sup>[\[43\]](#cite_note-46)</sup> and others have suggested even later. The foundation date of Chalcedon is itself subject to some debate; while many sources place it in 685 BC,<sup>[\[44\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTELister197935-47)</sup> others put it in 675 BC<sup>[\[45\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreely199610-48)</sup> or even 639 BC (with Byzantion's establishment placed in 619 BC).<sup>[\[42\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsaac1986199-45)</sup> Some sources refer to Byzantium's foundation as the 7th century BC.

[^cite_note-pop-figures-71]: &#x20;Historians disagree – sometimes substantially – on population figures of Istanbul (Constantinople), and other world cities, prior to the 20th century. A follow-up to Chandler & Fox 1974,[Chandler 1987](#CITEREFChandler1987), pp. 463–505<sup>[\[80\]](#cite_note-chfo-85)</sup> examines different sources' estimates and chooses the most likely based on historical conditions; it is the source of most population figures between 100 and 1914. The ranges of values between 500 and 1000 are due to [Morris 2010](#CITEREFMorris2010), which also does a comprehensive analysis of sources, including Chandler (1987); Morris notes that many of Chandler's estimates during that time seem too large for the city's size, and presents smaller estimates. Chandler disagrees with [Turan 2010](#CITEREFTuran2010) on the population of the city in the mid-1920s (with the former suggesting 817,000 in 1925), but Turan, p. 224, is used as the source of population figures between 1924 and 2005. Turan's figures, as well as the 2010 figure,<sup>[\[216\]](#cite_note-turkstat-curr-222)</sup> come from the Turkish Statistical Institute. The drastic increase in population between 1980 and 1985 is largely due to an enlargement of the city's limits (see the [Administration section](#Administration)). Explanations for population changes in pre-Republic times can be inferred from the [History section](#History).

[^cite_note-116]: &#x20;According to some sources, the conical roof of [Galata Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galata_Tower) was destroyed by a storm in 1875,<sup>[\[105\]](#cite_note-Galata_Tower_1-110)</sup> but photographs taken by foreign travelers such as James Robertson, Ernest De Caranza and Francis Bedford in the 19th century have revealed that the conical roof, which existed in 1854,<sup>[\[106\]](#cite_note-Galata_Tower_2-111)</sup><sup>[\[108\]](#cite_note-Robertson_1-113)</sup><sup>[\[109\]](#cite_note-Robertson_2-114)</sup><sup>[\[110\]](#cite_note-De_Caranza-115)</sup> was removed by 1862.<sup>[\[106\]](#cite_note-Galata_Tower_2-111)</sup><sup>[\[107\]](#cite_note-Galata_Tower_3-112)</sup> The photo taken by Francis Bedford on 21 May 1862<sup>[\[106\]](#cite_note-Galata_Tower_2-111)</sup><sup>[\[107\]](#cite_note-Galata_Tower_3-112)</sup> reveals that there were ongoing reparation (or modification) works in 1862, and the flag pole on top was also temporarily missing.<sup>[\[106\]](#cite_note-Galata_Tower_2-111)</sup><sup>[\[107\]](#cite_note-Galata_Tower_3-112)</sup> Either the "storm in 1875" had actually happened earlier, c. 1862,<sup>[\[106\]](#cite_note-Galata_Tower_2-111)</sup><sup>[\[107\]](#cite_note-Galata_Tower_3-112)</sup> or the uppermost part of the tower was simply modified or reconstructed c. 1862.<sup>[\[106\]](#cite_note-Galata_Tower_2-111)</sup><sup>[\[107\]](#cite_note-Galata_Tower_3-112)</sup> Another probability is that the conical roof which was reconstructed c. 1862 might have been destroyed a few years later by a storm in 1875.<sup>[\[105\]](#cite_note-Galata_Tower_1-110)</sup>

[^cite_note-232]: &#x20;Based on state register data, which is unchangeable and inherited from the family. A married women is also registered to her husband's province.

[^cite_note-uefa-category-323]: &#x20;UEFA does not apparently keep a list of Category 4 stadiums, but regulations stipulate that only these elite stadiums are eligible to host UEFA Champions League Finals,<sup>[\[313\]](#cite_note-320)</sup> which Atatürk Olympic Stadium did in 2005, and UEFA Europa League (formerly UEFA Cup) Finals,<sup>[\[314\]](#cite_note-321)</sup> which Şükrü Saracoğlu Stadium did in 2009. [Türk Telekom Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%BCrk_Telekom_Arena) is noted as an elite UEFA stadium by its architects.<sup>[\[315\]](#cite_note-322)</sup>
